Built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 (DMG sha256 `0930ce98…`, health 200, 148 quotes / 3063 materials / `integrity_check ok`, 0 errors and 0 CSP violations in the log). **#407 ships in the same install and is forward-fixing by construction**: saved quotes keep their existing `result`, and the export columns read from it, so a file exported after this install is correct without re-saving anything.
>
> **Sprint S-EXPORT-SETUP-GAP — the CSV column that was 5% light on every row since it shipped, 2026-09-22 (closing SHA: `097c5e3` via PR #407, with `dd0d1aa` via PR #406).** Henry exported a quote, added the cost columns up, and got **0.02338 against a G.Total of 0.02453**. The app was internally consistent — `TTL.MAT + PROCESS + TOOLING + PACK&SHIP` lands exactly on 0.02453 — so the discrepancy was in the export, not the arithmetic. **The missing 0.00115 is `bd_setup_mach + bd_setup_labor`, a cluster with no column anywhere**: it is inside G.Total and nowhere else, so nothing on the row could account for it. **Root cause is a field name that means less than it says**: `calcEngine.js:1229-1230` writes `overhead: overhead - setup_mach_total` and `labor_cost: labor_cost - setup_labor_total`, making both RUN-ONLY, while `bd_overhead`/`bd_labor` carry the full figures — and the export read the run-only pair. **This is MES-3-FIX-47 one surface over.** That ticket fixed the identical defect on the KPI strip in May, via `kpiBuckets.js`; the CSV kept it for four more months because fixing one reader of a misnamed field does not surface the others (Lesson 21, amended). `bd_overhead + bd_labor` is exactly the strip's PROCESS bucket, so the two screens now agree about one quote instead of differing by the setup cluster. **Measured before shipping rather than assumed, and the measurement is what makes it a P1 rather than a nit**: all 148 live quotes carry `bd_overhead` and `bd_labor`, and **all 148 have a non-zero setup cluster**, so every row of every export ever taken was short — by up to **8.76/pc on quote 183**. All four Complex quotes check out too, and the Cpx synthetic aggregate had to learn the two fields, or a Complex quote with no FG sub-product would have started exporting **blank cells** — a fix creating a second defect. **Henry chose merging setup into the two existing columns** over adding separate Setup Mach / Setup Labor columns, knowing the trade it carries: `Overhead` in the CSV now means setup-inclusive while `Overhead` in the on-screen Detailed Breakdown still means run-only. A one-line rename to `Overhead (incl. setup)` would remove the ambiguity but changes the CSV header, so it was raised rather than taken. **The five cells and the total now come from one helper**, which is the part that outlives this fix: the decomposition was wrong from the day the columns shipped and nothing caught it, because **a column that is merely too small looks exactly like a column that is right** — a blank one gets reported the same day. The fixture is quote 214's real numbers for the same reason; a fixture chosen to be round would not have caught a 5% shortfall either. **A KNOWN RESIDUAL GAP is kept visible instead of papered over.** `s_ttl` also includes `vat_loss + proc_extra_vat + proc_extra` (`calcEngine.js:1142`) and the export has a column for none of the three. All three are zero on all 148 live quotes, so the columns do sum today — but a quote that ever carries one will be short again, by design rather than by accident. `costColumnsGap()` and its test exist so that arrives as a documented limitation rather than as the next "the export is wrong" report. **#406 `dd0d1aa`** recorded the hardware verification the S-TIER-VIEW entry was missing: Henry opened `RFQ-2026-S0063`, whose active tier is MOQ 3 rather than MOQ 1 — the sharper case, since all three surfaces open on MOQ 3 and moving only the Cost Structure picker to MOQ 1 leaves the summary bar visibly behind. It did, and the server log shows **zero `POST`/`PATCH /quotes` across the whole session of switching tiers** with quote 203 still carrying `active_moq_idx = 2` afterwards. The lint guard asserts the source contains no write; that shows no write happened — different claims, and only the second is what the operator cares about. **Verify**: client **2144 → 2152 pass**; ESLint 0 errors with the 4 warnings on `Summarize.jsx` measured identical on `main` by stashing the same files; prettier clean; build green; CI 10/10 on both.
>
> **Sprint S-TIER-VIEW — a picker that looks without touching, a remark that stops saying nothing, and the hole that does not exist yet, 2026-09-21 (closing SHA: `19ab614` via PR #404, with `f53f247` #402 and `72220e1` #403).** **#402 — `Library/Fleet/` held back at DIRECTORY level, and the level is the decision.** Today that directory holds only `heartbeats.json` (hashed fingerprints and hostnames, no customer name). But it is also where `pending-licenses.json` appears the first time the fleet distribution flow is used, and that file carries **FULL SIGNED LICENCES for every other machine** — exactly what #399 had just held back, multiplied by the fleet. A per-file exclude would not have caught it, because the filename differs from `license.json*`; the hole would have opened **silently on the day that feature first ships**, with nothing to announce it. Excluding the directory covers whatever `fleetStore.js` adds to it next, which is the whole argument for the coarser rule. The cost is real and small, and was measured rather than assumed: `fleetStore.js:90` reads `readJson(path, {})`, so after a disk loss the fleet table comes back **empty rather than broken**, and `POST /api/heartbeat` refills it as machines check in — only `first_seen` and the row of a machine that never checks in again are gone for good. **The second exclude in that PR is a miss in my own audit, not a new decision**: I had reported the fingerprint as living in `heartbeats.json`, and it also sits raw in `<userData>/installation-id`, which I did not see because that audit only grepped `*.json`. It costs nothing to drop — `resolveInstallationId()` treats a cache miss by recomputing `sha256(machineId)` and re-caching, identical on the same hardware, and on different hardware the licence needs re-minting anyway. A full sweep of the share for the fingerprint went **1 file → 0**. **#403 `72220e1` — the remark printed lines that said nothing.** Every checked material produced a bullet, including ones whose Clear Materials MOQ could not be computed: `- UTP50-4: —`. Two of the nine lines on the quote Henry was reading were exactly that, and that block is the **CUSTOMER-facing** text, where a dash states nothing anyone can act on. `clear_pcs` is null whenever the conversion fails — no NPI match, or `qpa_m2 = 0` — and is deliberately never fabricated, so its absence is the signal to drop the line. **Nothing is hidden from the operator, and that split is the point**: the row stays checked and the Materials MOQ table immediately below still shows it with the dash, so a missing conversion remains visible to whoever is quoting; only the sentence that would have gone out empty is removed. `CalcLegend` documented the old format **including `- MAT-LINER: —` in its worked example**, and was updated in both languages — a manual that disagrees with the app is worse than none, because the reader sees no dash line on screen and has no way to tell which is wrong (#376's rule, one surface over). Three existing tests pinned the em-dash and **went red for the right reason** before being rewritten, which is the evidence the change actually took effect. **#404 `19ab614` — the Cost Structure picker, and the inconsistency is deliberate.** Cost Structure and the Detailed Breakdown under it always showed the active tier, while the Cost Breakdown tab offered no way to change it — the table at the top lists every tier's KPIs side by side, but seeing MOQ 2's structure meant leaving the tab. It is now a dropdown, and the Detailed Breakdown follows it. **Henry ruled it VIEW-ONLY** when the two readings were put to him: `active_moq_idx` is saved with the quote and drives the summary bar, every other tab, the margin hold and which tier's aggregates count as the quote's own, so "let me look at MOQ 2" must not re-point the quote at MOQ 2. **The summary-bar dropdown from #397 IS global**, so the two behave differently on purpose — which is precisely why it needs a guard: a later tidy-up pass reaching for consistency would turn a view action into one that edits saved data, with no test red and no error. `tierSelect.lint.test.js` asserts neither file writes `active_moq_idx`, and sabotaging the picker to dispatch `SET_ACTIVE_MOQ` reddens **exactly that one test** and no other. **It does follow downward**, because both controls sit on one screen and a local view contradicting a quote-level statement is the worse failure; `planTierViewSync` tells "the operator picked another tier" from "the active tier moved underneath us" by remembering which active index the view was last aligned with — without that they are the same mismatch and following would fight every pick the moment it was made. Two quieter guards: the options are built from **`tiers`, the same array both cards read**, so the list cannot offer a tier the cards cannot show (Lesson 41); and `clampTierIdx` covers a tier deleted on another sub-tab while this one is open, where `tiers[stale]` is `undefined` and renders a **blank card rather than throwing**. A single-tier quote gets no dropdown — 112 of 147 are single-tier. **ESLint caught a real defect, not a nit**: the Complex hook was first placed after that file's `if (!lib)` early return, a conditional hook call that would have broken React's hook ordering on any render where the library had not loaded. **A claim of mine that was wrong and is recorded rather than smoothed over**: after Henry reported the Cpx tier surviving a save-and-reopen, I checked the server log, found the last `PATCH /quotes` predated the install, and told him the round-trip had been in-session; later, seeing the quote count move 147 → 148, I told him the Cpx save had reached the server after all. **It had not** — the two saves that landed (`POST` then `PATCH /quotes/214`) are a **standard** quote, and the newest Cpx row's `updated_at` is the bulk-reconcile stamp every row shares, not a save of its own. The first measurement was right and the second reading of it was careless. The conclusion is unchanged and rests on the in-session test, which is sufficient for this specific bug class: a write mis-landed in `stdState` would leave `CplxSummaryBar` reading an unchanged `cs.active_moq_idx` and the row frozen, so a row that moves IS the routing proof — and persistence was never in question, since 19 pre-existing quotes already carry a non-zero index and the save path is untouched. **Verify**: client **2123 → 2144 pass** across #403 and #404 (10 tier-view helpers, 10 source-level, 1 remark); ESLint 0 errors with the warnings on touched files measured identical on `main` by stashing the same files; prettier clean; build and CI 10/10 on all three. Built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 (DMG sha256 `1bfaef9b…`, health 200, 148 quotes / 3063 materials / `integrity_check ok`, 0 errors and 0 CSP violations in the log). Markers measured against the installed bundle FIRST per Lesson 43, **with a control**: `sc-cb-tier-select` and `cb.tier_view_aria` read 0 installed and 3 after, while `sc-sumbar-tier-select` read **2 both times** — the control is what shows the two zeroes were a real absence and not a grep pointed at the wrong directory. Each mirror exclude was verified by running the job **twice**: the first run logged `REMOVED` and still reported OK, the second logged nothing, which is what distinguishes an exclude that stops the file going up from a sweep cleaning it off afterwards. **Confirmed on hardware the next morning, and the measurement is better evidence than the guard.** Henry opened `RFQ-2026-S0063` — a quote whose active tier is MOQ 3, not MOQ 1, which made it the sharper test: all three surfaces open on MOQ 3, so moving only the Cost Structure picker to MOQ 1 leaves the summary bar visibly behind. It did. What settles it is not the screenshot but the server log: **zero `POST`/`PATCH /quotes` across the whole session of switching tiers**, and quote 203 still carries `active_moq_idx = 2` afterwards. The lint guard asserts the source contains no write; this shows no write happened. Those are different claims, and only the second one is what the operator actually cares about. Log also clean — 0 errors, 0 CSP violations, 0 client_error.
>
> **Sprint S-MIRROR-HOLDBACK — what a shared drive should not carry, and the installer that would have undone the decision, 2026-09-21 (closing SHA: `a147fed` via PR #400, with `2ccdb1a` via PR #399).** **#399 — the licence came off the departmental share.** Henry's call, following the finding reported at the end of S-OPS-HOUSEKEEPING rather than acted on unilaterally. A licence is **not a credential**: it is signed, breaks if edited, and is useless on another machine because `installation_id` is `sha256(machineId)` bound to that machine's hardware. What it does on a shared drive is publish this box's fingerprint, the **customer name**, tier and expiry to everyone who can open the folder. **Two lists, not one, and that is the load-bearing choice.** Folding it into `SECRET_EXCLUDES` would have been one line shorter and wrong: the post-run sweep counts credential hits into `LEAKS`, and `LEAKS > 0` sets `RC=99`, turning the Settings → Backup card red. A licence landing is not an incident, and an alarm that fires for non-incidents is the one people stop reading — the exact failure the card was built to avoid (Lesson 40, Lesson 0). It gets its own `HELD` counter, its own message, and leaves `RC` alone. **The sweep is also the cleanup, which is the part that is easy to get wrong**: `rsync --delete` does **not** remove a file already on the destination once it is excluded — rsync stops considering it at all — so the exclude alone would have left both files there forever while the log said OK. `--delete-excluded` would have changed that for **every** exclude at once; sweeping by name removes exactly the held-back files and leaves `--delete` semantics untouched. **Verified by running it twice, and the second run is the one that proves anything**: the first logged both `REMOVED` and still reported **OK, not ERROR**; the second logged **nothing**, which is what shows the exclude stops them going up rather than the sweep cleaning up after they land. That gap is real on a shared drive — a run that dies midway leaves the file sitting there, which is how a live `OPS_TOTP_KEY` sat on this same share for 13 minutes on 2026-09-16. Share now holds 0 `license.json` and 0 credential files across 532 files, its `ops.db` still opens with 147 quotes and `integrity_check ok`, and the local licence plus both backups are untouched. **#400 `a147fed` — the installer would have quietly reversed a deliberate hold.** `install.sh` ends in `launchctl load`, and it writes `<LABEL>.plist` while the hold is expressed as `<LABEL>.plist.disabled-<date>` — a **different filename**. So it never saw the hold and never overwrote it: it created a fresh enabled agent beside it. Its own header even instructs you to re-run it after editing the script, which is the harmful step. **The damage would have been silent and specifically shaped**: a `launchd` job cannot read a share the Finder mounted, and its own `mount_smbfs` is refused because the Finder already holds that share, so every scheduled run takes `write_status error` — **not `skip`** — reddening the Backup card every four hours while manual runs keep writing `ok`. **A health signal that flickers is worse than one steadily wrong**, because nobody can tell which state to believe, and that card exists to be believed. **The root cause is not the installer**: the rename convention was invented on 2026-09-17 and recorded only in the prose of Lesson 44, while `uninstall.sh` deletes the plist outright — so nothing in the tooling knew the rename meant anything. It is machine-readable now AND written into the folder's README where an operator looks. The installer still does the half that is always safe (update the script), refreshes the held plist in place so it is not stale when re-enabled, and refuses to load while **printing the reason and the escape hatch** rather than a bare refusal; `OPS_FORCE_ENABLE_AGENT=1` overrides, following the `OPS_SKIP_*` convention the build scripts already use. **The test drives the REAL installer** against a throwaway `HOME` with `launchctl` stubbed on `PATH`, so no branch can touch this machine's launchd — and it lives in `scripts/` because the CI glob is `server/**` + `scripts/**`; under `deploy/` it would never run, which is the same class of mistake as a guard nobody executes. Its fifth case is **the control**: with no marker a normal install must still load, or a guard that simply always refused would pass every other assertion while quietly breaking installs on a fresh machine. Sabotage-checked — removing the guard reddens exactly the three disabled-case tests and leaves the two normal-path ones green. **Reported and deliberately NOT fixed, the second one being the more valuable.** (1) `data/Library/Fleet/heartbeats.json` also carries hardware fingerprints — two machines, with hostnames, tier and expiry, but **no customer name**; `fleetStore.js:90` reads it as `readJson(path, {})` so a missing file yields an empty fleet table rather than an error, and `POST /api/heartbeat` refills it, meaning the only permanent loss is `first_seen` and the row of any machine that never checks in again. Not worth holding back for a hashed fingerprint. (2) **`pending-licenses.json` in that same directory holds FULL SIGNED LICENCES for other machines** — exactly what #399 just held back, multiplied by the fleet. It does not exist yet because the distribution flow has not been used, and **the new `license.json*` exclude would NOT catch it**, because the filename differs. So a correct decision today leaves a hole of precisely that size for the day that feature is first used, and nothing would announce it. The moment to close it is before the file appears, by excluding `/data/Library/Fleet/`; the cost is the fleet history above. Henry's call. **A process slip of mine, recorded**: the first CI wait loop exited as soon as **one** check passed rather than when none were pending, so it reported done while half the suite was still running — the same shape as Lesson 40's "did it run" versus "did it succeed", made by the person who had just finished writing that distinction down twice. **Verify**: server **1221 → 1226 pass**, client 2123; `bash -n` clean on both scripts; prettier clean; CI 10/10 on both PRs. #400 was also run live on this box: the script updated, the agent stayed unloaded, no enabled plist appeared, and the held plist came out byte-identical.
>
> **Sprint S-TIER-DROPDOWN — switch MOQ tier where the numbers are, and a licence dash that was mine, 2026-09-21 (closing SHA: `d8220e4` via PR #397, with `69aa2ca` via PR #396).** **#397 — the TIER cell was a read-only badge**, so changing which MOQ tier is active meant leaving whatever sub-tab you were on, going back to RFQ & MOQ Info, ticking a radio, and navigating back. It is a dropdown now, each option carrying its quantity (`MOQ 2 · 5,000`). Not an edge case, measured before building: of 147 live quotes **35 carry more than one tier** (up to six) and **19 are saved with a non-zero active tier**. A single-tier quote keeps the badge — 112 of the 147 are single-tier, and a dropdown holding one option is a control that cannot act. **The load-bearing decision is which field the list comes from.** `extra_moqs` is what the RFQ radios iterate (`CalcHeader.jsx:417`, `ComplexCalc.jsx:1673`), so the dropdown iterates it too; deriving the same set from `num_moq` instead would let the dropdown offer a tier the radio does not, and selecting it would open an empty tier — Lesson 41's shape, a control listing options that select nothing. Across all 147 quotes the two agree **today**, so this costs nothing now; the drift is what is being closed, and a test pins it by feeding a state claiming five tiers while holding data for two. **The shared bar dispatches nothing**, because Std and Cpx write the same field through DIFFERENT actions: `SET_ACTIVE_MOQ` lands in `stdState` while Cpx must route through `SET_CPLX_FIELD` or the write is lost on save (**MES-3-FIX-53**, which cost 36 days of silent data loss in May). Each wrapper supplies its own setter; `ComplexMoqTab` already owns one for the radios but is a sibling component unreachable from the bar, so `ComplexCalc` builds one on its existing `setCplxField`. **The guard went red on its first run against its own rationale** — a bare `/SET_ACTIVE_MOQ/` over `ComplexCalc.jsx` matched the comment explaining why Complex must not use it. Narrowed to code lines only, with a sabotage test proving the narrowing did not blind it (see Lesson 46). **#396 `69aa2ca` — the em-dash was my substitution, not a request.** The licence minted that morning read `CCL Design Vietnam — Hai Phong` (U+2014) where Henry had typed a plain hyphen. `customer` is in `SIGNED_FIELDS`, so it could only be corrected by re-minting: same `installation_id`, tier M / max*users 20, expiry 2029-06-09, six features, only `customer` and `issued_at` moved. Verified with the app's OWN verifier **plus a tamper control** — one extra character gives `bad-signature`, which is what makes the `ok: true` mean anything — and the previous licence kept beside it. **The fix in the PR is the source, not the symptom**: `mint-license.command:21` holds a `CUSTOMER` default that seeds every future mint, and it held the em-dash; three docs and the signer's own docstring modelled the same string, so the next person to mint would have repeated it. Five occurrences, four files, and the tool's default is now byte-identical to the installed licence. Two hits deliberately untouched: `INTERNAL_TRUST_SETUP.md:81` is a Windows Authenticode certificate subject whose doc must keep matching the real cert, and `SLIP_PROPOSAL_2026-05-24.md` is a dated record saying Yen Phong, which #391 established stays as written. **Operational the same day — the S-ELECTRON-HARDENING gap closed by hand.** That entry listed what CI could not cover: "the IPC paths reachable only after login — printing, file dialogs, the drawing PDF preview, Settings → About". All four were exercised on the installed build. The PDF preview passed, which matters most because pdf.js holds the only eval-adjacent code in the app and was now running under `script-src 'self'`; confirmed not by report alone but by finding `GET /layout/Anam_VQB1A2413*…pdf`in the server log with **zero CSP violations** beside it. The native Save dialog appeared for CSV export — the tell that the IPC bridge lives, since the browser fallback would still produce a file and look like success. **The printer result read as a failure and was not**: Diagnostics showed`✓ 0 máy in (87ms)`, and macOS itself answers `lpstat: No destinations added`— the box has no printers, so the bridge reported the truth. A dead bridge would have shown ✗, not a successful empty list; the other seven Diagnostics rows, each a different`window.ops`channel, all answered too, which is stronger evidence for`sandbox: true`than the four steps written for it. **And one report corrected by measurement rather than accepted**: "saved and reopened, the tier held" could not have gone through the server — the last`PATCH /quotes`was 09:41 ICT against an install at 12:00, so that round-trip was in-session. It still proves the Cpx routing, because a write mis-landed in`stdState`would leave`CplxSummaryBar`reading an unchanged`cs.active_moq_idx`and the row frozen; and persistence was never in question, since 19 existing quotes already carry a non-zero index and the save path is untouched. Cpx was then confirmed by hand. **Verify**: client **2099 → 2123 pass** (13 helper + 11 source-level); ESLint 0 errors and the one warning on the touched files measured on`main`by stashing the same files — pre-existing, none added; prettier clean; build and perf budget green; CI 10/10 on both. Built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 (DMG sha256`807a74e4…`, health 200, 147 quotes / 3063 materials / `integrity_check ok`). Markers chosen the Lesson-43 way with a control: `sc-sumbar-tier-select`and`sumbar.tier_select_aria`measured **0 on the installed bundle first**, then 2 after, while`sc-sumbar-tier-badge` read **2 both times** — the control is what shows the two zeroes were a real absence and not a grep pointed at the wrong directory.
>
> **Sprint S-OPS-HOUSEKEEPING — the copies that had quietly stopped matching, 2026-09-21 (closing SHA: `5c27fd0` via PR #393, with `7e7f75e` #389, `43e8d12` #390, `9baae80` #391 and `1b5486a` #392).** Five changes, and **three of them are the same defect in different clothes**: content that lives in two places where only one gets edited. **#389 — the audit toolkit is committed**, `reports/` gitignored, and `FINDINGS-2026-09-20.md` keeps its original text with a dated status block on top. The block exists mainly for its finding 4, which recommends binding the server to `127.0.0.1`: a document sitting in the repo telling the next reader to do that is a hazard whether or not they also find the sprint entry, so the refusal and the six-LAN-IP measurement are recorded **where the recommendation is**. It also corrects three details the report got wrong — the real multer fix is ≥2.3.0, `fast-uri`/`js-yaml` DO ship, and a CSP already existed. Its CI red on arrival was mine: `format:check` runs prettier over **everything** while lint-staged only formats js/json/md/css, so a `.yml` reached CI unformatted. Before letting prettier touch a Semgrep rules file I diffed it — only `message:` quote style moved, no `pattern:` and the same 12 rule ids — because reformatting a rules file could otherwise change what it matches. **#390 — the licence said Yen Phong and the customer is Hai Phong.** `customer` is in `SIGNED_FIELDS`, so this cannot be fixed by editing `license.json`: `canonicalize()` would produce different bytes and the app would answer `bad-signature`. Re-minted with the same `installation_id`, tier and expiry — only `customer` and `issued_at` differ — verified with the app's OWN verifier against the embedded public key BEFORE install and again from the live file after, and the previous licence kept beside it. Other machines keep their old name until each is re-minted; the name is cosmetic on the card, not a gate. **#391 — three names for one site** (Yen Phong 49 hits, Hai Duong 65, Hai Phong 10), settled on Hai Phong across **30 files, 67 insertions against 67 deletions**, all prose and comments. **The dry run is what made it safe**: walking the filesystem put build artefacts and the gitignored 30 MB NPI parts snapshot on the list, where `Hai Duong` is customer DATA — switching the source to `git ls-files` dropped seven such paths before anything was written. Left alone deliberately: the frozen-quote fixtures, where `Hai Duong` is a site KEY in `sga_rate_pct_by_site` and changing a byte would break the 20-year reproducibility contract for a cosmetic reason; every dated record; and one sentence in `mint-license.command` whose meaning depends on the old word. **#392 — `.agents/rules/context.md` is CONTEXT.md plus frontmatter, and I had drifted it myself**: both sections added to CONTEXT.md that day went into the source and not the mirror, thirty lines, nothing red. The site rename DID reach both because that was a sweep over every tracked file; the hand-written sections did not, because those were edits to one file. Same day the lesson about exactly this was written. The fix is a guard, not a resync — it names the first differing line and which side is ahead, and it lives under `scripts/` so CI actually runs it. **#393 — the off-site mirror refused to run against a share that was mounted and writable.** `DEST_VOLUME` was the single path `/Volumes/Henry`; connect to the share ROOT instead and it lands at `/Volumes/Departments$` with the mirror two levels down. The job read that as unreachable, tried to self-mount, and smbfs refused because Finder already held the share — a failure its own comment had predicted. Candidates are listed now and the one whose SENTINEL is readable wins, because an unmounted share leaves an empty directory of the same name and a stale path must not beat a live mount; verified with both mounted, a probe file written through one path appearing through the other. **And the find that outweighs the patch**: there are two copies of that script and the repo held **156 lines still pointing at `/Volumes/OPSBACKUP`**, the drive that never succeeded in 84 days, while the installed copy had 207 lines of working self-mount code from 2026-09-17. `install.sh` copies repo over installed — running it would have replaced a working backup job with the one that never worked, silently. Both are byte-identical now and the header says to edit the repo copy. **Operational**: the mirror had not succeeded since 2026-09-17 and was brought current — 5.5G / 533 files, and the mirrored `ops.db` opens with 147 quotes and `integrity_check ok`. A credential sweep of what actually landed found no `.env`, no `users.json`, no `sessions.json`, no `totp_secrets`, no Library tarballs — and **two `license.json` files**, which are signed artefacts rather than secrets but do expose the hardware fingerprint and customer on a shared drive. Reported for Henry to rule on rather than removed, because a mirror that cannot restore a licence is a worse trade if the disk dies.
>
> **Sprint S-ELECTRON-HARDENING — four permissive defaults, one of them overriding a policy that was already correct, 2026-09-21 (closing SHA: `7cf9af2` via PR #394, with `fb7d5bc` #386, `0646f9e` #387 and `854f9bf` #388).** The configuration half of the audit, taken in the order that made each step verifiable. **#386 first, because it is the runtime**: Electron 41.3.0 carries three HIGH advisories and unlike the electron-builder findings it SHIPS to every machine. The vulnerable range ends at 41.10.2, so **41.10.7 clears it without a major bump** — and a major bump is the Lesson 28 trap, because `better-sqlite3` exists twice in the packaged app and an ABI change makes the embedded server resolve the wrong one. Measured rather than assumed: NMV **145 before and after**, confirmed by loading better-sqlite3 under the new runtime and running a real insert. **#387 — no permission handler existed at all**, so Electron granted whatever a page asked for. The app asks for exactly one thing, writing to the clipboard, and never reads it back, so the honest allowlist is one entry and `clipboard-read` is not on it. Set on `defaultSession` because the two windows that would suffer most are the wizard and first-run dialog, which still run with `nodeIntegration`. The same PR routed all three `shell.openExternal` call sites through one guard allowing http and https only — the OS launches whatever app claims a scheme, so `file:` opens an app and `smb:` mounts a share. Both decisions live in `utils/rendererPolicy.js` so they can be asserted without booting Electron and so main.js holds ONE definition of each. **A finding worth more than that patch**: `desktop/utils/*.test.js` was run by NOTHING — `netProbe.test.js` and `serverIdentity.test.js` had been sitting unexecuted, so adding a file there would have joined two tests nobody runs. Both still passed, but neither was protecting anything; `test:utils` now globs them and the root chain calls it, so all 19 run in CI. **#388 — `sandbox: false` on the main window was annotated "Cần false để preload truy cập node modules"**, which had stopped being true once every filesystem call moved behind IPC. The comment outlived the change that made it wrong, which is the ordinary way a security exception survives. The failure mode is the hidden one — flip the flag, the app still opens, and every `window.ops` call is simply gone — so the guard loads the REAL preload under the REAL flags and fetches a value produced in the main process back through `window.ops.app.getConfig()`, which cannot pass by accident, while pinning that `require`/`process`/`module`/`Buffer` stay undefined in the renderer. One prediction of mine was wrong in a useful direction: `process.versions.node` was expected to disappear under sandbox and does not, so Settings → About loses nothing — asserted now, because having checked once is not a guard. **#394 — the CSP was not missing, it was OVERRIDING a correct one.** main.js injects its policy by REWRITING the response header, so `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' 'unsafe-eval'` REPLACED the `script-src 'self'` the server has been serving the web surface all along. The desktop app — the one with an embedded server and a local database — was the weaker surface. Each keyword was measured before removal: the built `index.html` has two scripts both with `src=`, **zero** inline scripts and **zero** inline handlers; the three runtime `createElement('script')` sites are React preload APIs and all set `src`; the bundle has **zero** `eval(` and `new Function(`, the only `Function(` hits being a property named `toSJISFunction` and **pdf.js's own CSP feature detection**, which exists so it can take the non-eval path. `report-uri /api/csp-report` ships in the same change and is the half that makes it safe: a too-strict CSP does not throw, a screen just renders wrong, and that endpoint already `console.warn`s into main.log. **Verify**: desktop sandbox 5/5 + wizard 4/4 + firstrun 4/4 + license 17/17 + manifest 3/3 + utils 19/19; server 1221 pass / 1 chronic skip; client 2099; CI 10/10 on all four. Two DMGs built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with markers measured against the installed bundle FIRST per Lesson 43 — electron **41.3.0 → 41.10.7** with the `better_sqlite3.node` pair sha256-identical either side, `sandbox: false` **3 → 2** (the wizard and first-run windows keep it, which is what shows exactly one of three moved), `unsafe-eval` **1 → 0**. **Verified on the running server, not only in CI**: health 200, the renderer up with `--enable-sandbox` and a seatbelt client, the login page rendering through the strict policy, a login answering 200, and **zero CSP violations and zero errors** in the log since install. **Not covered and stated rather than implied**: the IPC paths reachable only after login — printing, file dialogs, the drawing PDF preview, Settings → About. The PDF preview is the one to check first: it touches all three changes at once, and pdf.js is where the only eval-adjacent code lives.
>
> **Sprint S-AUDIT-HARDENING — a security audit whose own deferral rationale turned out to be wrong, 2026-09-20 (closing SHA: `82a7ce5` via PR #383, with `b6b84a4` via PR #381 and `125735f` via PR #382).** Henry built an `audit/` toolkit — Semgrep rules written for this stack, `npm audit` across all three packages, Electronegativity — and ran it for the first time. Its three release-blocking findings were all REAL, and reviewing them turned up more than the tool reported. **The recommendation that had to be refused, first because it is the one that could have done damage**: the report proposed binding the server to `127.0.0.1` instead of `0.0.0.0`. The box's own audit log holds **six distinct LAN IPs across 199 events** (`10.102.3.60` ×133, `10.102.5.5` ×29, `10.102.3.252` ×17, `10.102.3.87` ×17, `10.102.3.61` ×2, `10.102.3.93` ×1). Binding to localhost takes every operator machine offline. The report did hedge it — _"trừ khi chủ ý dùng chung LAN"_ — but filed it under _should fix_, and a hedge inside a recommendation is not a safeguard; measuring the claim before acting on it is. **#381 `b6b84a4` — one XSS was reachable and the other was not, and the tool could not tell them apart.** Both wizard renderers run with `nodeIntegration: true` / `contextIsolation: false`, so anything parsed as HTML has full Node access, and both were flagged HIGH. `clientFirstRun.js` has **no sink at all** — `textContent` only, zero interpolations — so its HIGH is theoretical. `setupWizard.js:135` built its alert with `innerHTML` from a `msg` variable, and tracing every caller found the licence path **closed** (`tier` passes a `VALID_TIERS` allowlist before it is concatenated into `detail`) but `ops:setup.initDb` **open**: it returns `e.message` from `fs.mkdirSync(dataPath)`, Node embeds the operator-typed path verbatim, and that reached `innerHTML`. Fixed by building DOM instead of markup — `textContent` cannot parse, so there is nothing left to escape and no escaper to get wrong later. **The guard drives the real wizard HTML in a real BrowserWindow**, and restoring the `innerHTML` line turns it red with _the payload must not become an `<img>` element_ — the old code genuinely built the element, so that is a reproduction rather than an argument. Severity stated honestly rather than inflated: every route to it needs the person at the keyboard to paste the payload into their own wizard, so it is a structural hole, not a live incident — but one future handler echoing a server response would make the same sink remote. **#382 `125735f` — the bump, and the ticket that was wrong twice.** `npm audit` puts the vulnerable range at **`<=2.2.0`**, so MES-3-FIX-59 naming 2.2.0 as the fix was true on 2026-06-19 and false since; the real fix is 2.3.0 and 2.4.0 was installed. Root runtime audit went 2 HIGH → 1 HIGH, the survivor being `brace-expansion` under `exceljs/archiver`, which needs `--force` and was left alone. The report itself under-scoped the desktop side: its full audit is 14 high + 1 CRITICAL, but those are **electron-builder devDependencies** that never ship — `--omit=dev` leaves 4, and the report named 2 of them while missing that **`electron` 41.3.0 itself carries three HIGH advisories** and does ship. **The coverage gap is why the ticket sat**: it asked for a hand smoke of eight upload routes on a DMG, and only ONE had any multipart test. A hand smoke is also a one-shot — it proves nothing about the next bump. **#383 `82a7ce5` — what the automated sweep found in its first run.** MES-3-FIX-59 deferred for three months on the rationale that all eight multer instances sat behind `requireRole(4)` / `requireTabAccess(...)` / `isSys(u)` gates. **Six do. Two did not.** `/api/backup/upload` and `/api/import-xlsm` ran multer FIRST and checked the session inside the handler, so an unauthenticated caller made the server parse a multipart body and spool it to disk before being refused — 200 MB on one, and 50 MB on the other, which carried **no rate limit at all**. On a server listening `0.0.0.0` for six LAN clients, that is precisely the surface the advisories describe, and it was open the whole time the bump was deferred **because the rationale for deferring was never checked**. The gates reproduce the handlers' own checks exactly rather than inventing policy — `isSys(u)` is `roleLevel >= 5` so `requireRole(5)` matches; import-xlsm 401s without a session and 403s `viewonly` so `authMiddleware` + `requireRole(2)` matches — and both in-handler checks are KEPT, because this moves the first refusal earlier, it does not relocate the decision. **The #382 guard reported its own fix**: written to flag movement in BOTH directions, it failed with `actual: []` against the two known routes, which is the _"good: the two known ones were fixed"_ branch its own message predicted. The two tests then collapse into one, since an auth refusal IS proof the gate ran before the body was read — had multer gone first, the fileFilter would have answered 500 to a `.png`. **Verify**: server **1216 → 1219 pass** / 1 chronic skip, client 2099, desktop wizard 4/4 + firstrun 4/4 + license 17/17 + manifest 3/3; ESLint 0 errors with the warnings on touched files measured identical to `main`; CI 10/10 on all three. One DMG carries all three (sha256 `1e0aae23…`), built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with three markers measured against the installed bundle FIRST per Lesson 43 — multer **2.2.0 → 2.4.0**, `requireRole(5)` **0 → 1**, `box.textContent = msg` in `app.asar` **0 → 1** with the old `el.innerHTML = msg ?` **1 → 0**. **Then verified on the running production server rather than only in CI**: an anonymous multipart POST to `/api/import-xlsm` and `/api/backup/upload` now answers **401** where it used to parse the body first, `/health` stays 200, and 147 quotes are intact. The one thing not verified by this session is an AUTHENTICATED upload — that needs Henry's password, which is his to type.
>
> **Sprint S-TOOLLIFE-UNIT — the column that never said what it was counting, 2026-09-20 (closing SHA: `ad81e03` via PR #379).** Two days after S-TOOLING-DENOMINATOR shipped, Henry: _"tool life là số shot chứ không phải số pcs như EAU nên khi tính toán thì phải tính số pcs cho khuôn chính bằng tool life × layout. Bạn kiểm tra lại công thức hiện tại đã đúng chưa?"_ **The formula was already right** — `cav = isJig ? 1 : layout` and `goodPcsPerTool = tlife × cav × yield` have been in `calcProcess` since #375. What was missing is that **nothing on screen said which unit `tool_life` is in**. EAU counts PIECES, `tool_life` counts SHOTS, and the bridge is `× layout` — the one step an operator cannot infer from the number itself, which is why the question had to be asked at all. The failure it invites is silent and expensive: enter a piece count in that column and the tooling is understated by precisely the layout factor — **10× on a typical job** — with no error, no warning, and a perfectly plausible number on the screen. **The finding that made this more than a label change**: the Cpx header was a **hardcoded English literal** (`<th>Tool Life</th>`) while Std read `t('cgrid.proc.tool_life')`, so the twins were **structurally incapable of moving together** — adding the unit to Std alone would have left Cpx unlabelled in BOTH languages, found eventually by someone quoting a Complex job rather than by a test. Both now read the same key, pinned by `toolLifeUnit.lint.test.js`; restoring the literal reddens exactly the three twin assertions and nothing else (Lesson 48). **Four surfaces carry the unit** so none can contradict another: the two process grids, the DDL library panel where the number is first typed, and the xlsx `05 Processes` sheet (internal-variant only, read by the same people). The Legend states it on the formula line and at the head of its note — #376's rule, a manual that disagrees with the app is worse than none. **Verified against live data rather than a fixture**: quote #213 (RFQ-2026-S0065, saved by Henry 2026-09-19) — Pressplate 200,000 shot × 10 layout × 0.9 yield = 1,800,000 good pcs → 1 tool → 24.55/9,600 = 0.0025573; Knife/Wood 98.00/9,600 = 0.0102083; sum **0.0128**, matching the Tool strip in his screenshot to the last digit. **All 24 tooling rows across the 12 quotes he saved that day need exactly 1 tool** (ratio 0.01–0.07), so the multi-tool branch #377 added has not yet fired on real work. **Three of my own errors, recorded because two are the same class as the bug being fixed.** (1) The first verification script passed `scrap: 5` meaning 5%, but `scrap_pct` is stored as a **FRACTION** — `calcMatScrapFactor` does `y *= 1 - p.scrap_pct` — so 5 meant 500%, the yield floored at `safeYieldDivisor`'s 0.001, and the engine appeared to demand **2,667 tools**. A unit error, made while fixing a unit ambiguity. (2) The second read `proc.tool_cost` (25.60) on a row carrying `tool_cost_src='plate'`, so the engine had correctly used the Layout-assigned **24.55** and I reported a false mismatch — `effectiveToolCost` ignores the row's own cell entirely once a source is assigned, and the stale 25.60 left in the data is a trap for anyone checking by hand. (3) The new lint test first failed for the **wrong reason** (a double-escaped regex in my own helper), which reads identically to a product fault until you look. **Henry confirmed the 0.8 safety factor** (_"đúng hệ số an toàn là 0.8 tức là 80%"_), which closes the eauCap-vs-raw-EAU choice S-TOOLING-DENOMINATOR left open, **in favour of eauCap on both sides** — counting against raw EAU would delete that factor as a by-product. **Still open**: whether `tool_life` means physical shots (keep `× yield`, as now) or good-output shots (drop it, else scrap is subtracted twice). The two agree on every row of live data today and diverge only when demand outlives a tool. **Verify**: client **2095 → 2099 pass**, server 1216 pass / 1 chronic skip, exporter 185 pass; ESLint 0 errors with the 4 warnings on the touched files measured identical to `main` by stashing the same five files; prettier clean; CI 10/10. Built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 (DMG sha256 `436874ed…`, health 200, 147 quotes intact), markers measured against the installed bundle FIRST per Lesson 43 — `Tool Life (shot)` **0 → 2 files**, `Tuổi thọ khuôn (shot)` **0 → 1**, `cgrid.proc.tip_tool_life` **0 → 3**; bare `Tool Life` read **4** installed and was rejected as a marker. `app.asar` was byte-identical between the new DMG and the running app (`54f87739…`), which is what proved the install could not change the SERVER role.

> **Sprint S-TOOLING-DENOMINATOR — the tooling divisor counted the wrong pieces, and bought fractions of a die, 2026-09-18 (closing SHA: `caa4577` via PR #377, with `b95a66c` via PR #375 and `81ce97e` via PR #376).** All three started from one question Henry asked — _"nếu EAU/cavities × life cycle > tool life thì mẫu số đang lấy số nào?"_ — which could only be answered by reading `calcProcess`, and reading it turned up two faults. **#375 — the comparison was between two different units.** `tool_life × layout` is what a tool can PHYSICALLY produce; scrap comes out of that, so per GOOD piece it wears proportionally faster — the same reason `run_labor` and `extra`, a few lines up in the same function, already divide by the yield. Meanwhile `eauCap` is `eau × 0.8`, which is good-piece DEMAND, so `MIN(produced, demanded)` was choosing the smaller of two incomparable things. Capacity became `tool_life × layout × yield`; the cap side was left exactly alone, because that branch is a **1.25× policy floor** from the CCL tooling spec, not a wear model, and inflating it by scrap too would have changed what it means. **#377 — you cannot buy a third of a die.** Henry, reading the worked example: _"nếu khấu hao phần khuôn là 1.33 thì làm tròn lên 2 cho tôi rồi phân bổ / tổng số pcs"_. `cost / goodPcs` bills a fraction of a tool once the run outlives one; purchasing buys whole ones. Both branches then collapse into a single expression — `tools = max(1, ceil(eauCap / goodPcs))`, `tooling = cost × tools / eauCap` — and a tool that outlasts demand needs 1 and lands back on `cost / eauCap`, **exactly the old cap branch**, so the 0.8 floor survives instead of being deleted by a side effect. **The choice inside that, stated rather than buried**: the count is taken against `eauCap`, NOT raw EAU. Counting and dividing by raw EAU would hand a long-life tool `cost / EAU` instead of `cost / 0.8EAU` — 20% cheaper — which is that same safety factor removed as a by-product of a rounding change. The two readings agree on Henry's own example. **A claim of mine that was wrong and is recorded rather than smoothed over**: I reported the 0.8–1.0 × EAU band as UNDER-charging. It does the opposite — at `life × layout = 18,000` against EAU 20,000 the cap charges **825** where fractional amortisation wants **733**. I had compared it against buying whole tools, which is a different question and became #377. So the "two points" Henry asked me to fix were one real point plus one of my own errors. **Measured read-only on live data before each ship**, because this is the money path: #375 moved 92 of 352 tooling rows across 52 of 134 quotes (+11–26.5%); #377 moved 94 of 324 rows across 55 of 135 quotes, up to **1.8× the tooling and +3.2% of subtotal**. **The customer-facing artefact does not move** — the server never re-runs calcEngine and the xlsx reads the persisted `quote.result` — so this is what an operator sees on re-opening, not a re-quote. Henry chose to apply it to all quotes rather than gate it by date. **That makes it a DELIBERATE break of the 20-year reproducibility contract, and `loadFrozenQuote.test.js` carries its own playbook for exactly that**: all four frozen fixtures regenerated, the pre-change copies **KEPT** under `__fixtures__/pre-tooling-yield/`, and a migration test proving an archived 2026 quote still OPENS — no throw, no NaN, no dropped field. **The second half of that migration test earned itself on its first run**: it asserts the drift is CONFINED to the tooling chain and immediately flagged a field. That turned out to be `gm_after_sga`, which genuinely belongs to the chain and was missing from my allowlist — but a change to a shared divisor is exactly the kind that leaks sideways into materials or inks while every number still looks plausible. **Eleven golden expectations moved, and every one was derived by hand from its own fixture FIRST and only then compared against what the engine produced** — they agreed in all eleven (see Lesson 47). Each still asserts the property it was written for: a Jig ignores layout (10.31 vs 2.5775, still 4× apart), the DDL life resolves, the row override wins, and the 80% cap table's `Metal` and `Jig` rows are **unchanged**, which is what shows the cap branch was not disturbed. **#376 `81ce97e` — the Legend prints this formula to operators** and would have gone on describing one the app no longer runs; updated in both languages after each change, because a manual that disagrees with the app is worse than none — somebody computes a tooling cost by hand, gets a different number from the screen, and has no way to tell which is wrong. **Verify**: client **2082 → 2095 pass**, server 1216 pass / 1 chronic skip; ESLint 0 errors / 314 warnings identical to `main`; CI 10/10 on all three; each built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with markers measured against the installed bundle first — the old `if/else` branch **1 → 0** and the new single expression **0 → 1**, then `Math.ceil(k/` **0 → 1** and the Legend's new wording **0 → 1**.
>
> **Sprint S-SILENT-DEAD — four things that were dead and nothing said so, 2026-09-18 (closing SHA: `2031b0d` via PR #373, with `f116880` via PR #372).** Two PRs, one theme: **code that does nothing fails no test, breaks no build and raises no lint.** **#372 `f116880` — a tooltip that could never render, and a field nobody reads.** The EAU cell carried two nested ternaries and the inner one was unreachable: it tested `showEauWarn`, which is itself `hasTooling && EAU missing`, so by the time it was reached the outer branch had already matched on the missing EAU. The specific message S-INPUT-WARN wrote was **dead from the day it shipped**, and nothing failed, because an unreachable branch renders no worse than a reachable one — you simply get the other message. Identical in Std and Cpx. Order now favours the specific message, and **that is the point rather than tidiness**: with a tool cost on a process, a blank EAU does not merely block the tab — `calcProcess` amortises tooling over MOQ instead of EAU, which inflated a quote's tooling **20×** on 2026-06-12 and read as a product bug for a day. The decision moved into `eauTooltipKey` rather than staying in JSX, because **logic inside JSX cannot be asserted in this repo at all — and being unassertable is exactly how a branch stays dead without anyone noticing.** The same PR removed `target_contr`, written by two state factories and read by nobody (no reference in client, server, domains or scripts) while riding into every saved quote **and into the signed `_Schema` payload of every export**, implying a 25% target no one set; the suite was unchanged at 2075, which is the evidence it was inert. **Forward-fix only** — 94 of 134 saved quotes keep the field until re-saved, because stripping it would be a mutation on production data for a value nothing reads. **#373 `2031b0d` — two whole screens dead for eight days.** Henry opened Drop-Down Lists and got `Cost → lib-ddl crashed / e is not a function`. `const { t } = useI18n()` puts a FUNCTION in scope; LibDDL's cutter-cost editor was written as `tiers.map((t, i) => …)` long before the i18n work, so when **#303 (2026-09-10)** substituted the literals for `t('lib.cost_usd')` and three siblings, those calls began **invoking the tier object as a function**. Rendering any tiered cutter cost killed the tab. **Ruling myself out came first and by measurement, not reasoning**: the day's other work touched `calcEngine`, `priceSolver` and the margin-hold hook, none of which LibDDL imports — proven by building the tree at `ab850f6` and finding the LibDDL chunk **byte-identical at 23912B**, differing only in the import URL of a renamed shared chunk; `git log -L` then put it on #303. **The stack came from the app's own client-error telemetry** (`/api/telemetry/client-error`, five frames, landing in `~/Library/Logs/ops-control-desktop/main.log`) — byte offset 7493 of the shipped chunk pointed straight at `placeholder:e(\`lib.cost_usd\`)`inside`f.map((e,t)=>…)`. Sweeping for the pattern found a **second dead screen nobody had reported**: the Permission Groups matrix does `tabCatalog.map((t) => …)`and calls`t('pg.unset_hint')`inside it. **The guard is scope-aware because a bare regex is useless here** — it flags **eleven** sites, **nine of them false**, since`t(`after the arrow is not`t(`inside the body; bracket-matching leaves exactly the two real offenders, and sabotage-checking reproduces the original by file, line and offending call. **A third finding, reported and deliberately NOT fixed**:`core_od`is an object in every site's`ddl_sites.json` and the server handles it as one (`Object.entries(d.core_od || {})`), but LibDDL's `OBJECT_KEYS`omits it, so the section matches neither branch and hits`return null`— the Core OD table renders **nothing** in the DDL editor. Out of scope for a crash fix; raised rather than folded in. **Verify**: client 2071 → **2076 pass** across the two PRs; ESLint 0 errors / 314 warnings identical to`main`; CI 10/10 on both; each built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with a marker measured against the installed bundle first — `target_contr`**2 → 0**, and for the crash a behaviour-level one rather than a string, since the strings already existed: the buggy caller`placeholder:e(\`lib.cost_usd\`)`**1 → 0** with the correct`s(…)` caller at **2**. That pair is also why the bug survived review — the input beside it called the same key correctly.
>
> **Sprint S-MARGIN-HOLD-FIX — the hold was correct code in the wrong component, 2026-09-18 (closing SHA: `aba60ab` via PR #370).** Henry ticked **Contr% at 25.0**, changed a process on Materials & Process, and watched the subtotal move `0.96947 → 0.97564` while **SELL PRICE stayed $0.9630** and Contr% slid to **24.9** — the exact failure the tick exists to prevent. Ticking or not ticking made no difference. **Every test was green and the logic was right; the driver was in the wrong place.** `StandardCalc.jsx:454` is `switch (activeSubTab)` — sub-tabs mount **exclusively**, so the auto-apply effect, written inside `CalcCostBreakdown`, was unmounted _precisely while the operator is on Materials & Process changing the costs it exists to react to_. The tier strip he was watching there is rendered by the **calculator**, which stays mounted across every sub-tab — so that is where the driver belongs. It moved into `useMarginHold`, called from `StandardCalc` and `ComplexCalc`; when no metric is held the hook never calls `computeTiers`, so a quote without a tick pays for none of it. **Two theories were dropped by measuring rather than guessing.** The draft gate looked likely until `getStatus(undefined)` was read and returns `'draft'`, so an unsaved quote is not blocked. Units looked likelier still — a pinned `0.25` compared against a displayed `25.0` would produce exactly this symptom — until live data was read READ-ONLY: quote 200 holds `contr=0.249976`, `gm=-0.0067`, `va=0.3104`, matching the screenshot to the digit. Fractions throughout, theory dead, **one `sqlite3` query instead of an afternoon**. **Moving the driver exposed a second fault in the anti-spin guard**, and it is the more interesting one. `planAutoHold` remembered **the last price it had written** and refused to write that price again; short-lived, a remount cleared it, but in a component that never unmounts it means a price **typed by hand can never be corrected** — the operator types 0.95, the hold re-solves 0.9912 as before, the guard recognises its own last write and stays silent, and the metric sits wrong with nothing explaining why. It now compares against **the price the tier currently holds**: writing a price that is already there changes nothing, so the loop still has a real stop condition, while a hand-typed price no longer matches what the hold wants and gets corrected on the next pass. Strictly better than the version it replaced, and it needs no ref at all. **The guard that pins this is source-level, because no unit test could have caught it** — the code was correct, it simply never ran, so the only assertable fact is WHERE it runs. `useMarginHold.lint.test.js` asserts both calculators call the hook, neither Cost Breakdown tab does, and neither re-grows a `planAutoHold` of its own (one driver, never two) — the same approach as `libraryRefresh.lint` and `remarkBuilder.lint`. Watched failing for the right reason before being kept: putting `useMarginHold(` back into `CalcCostBreakdown` reddens exactly that assertion and nothing else. **Verify**: client **2066 → 2071 pass**, 0 fail; ESLint 0 errors / 314 warnings identical to `main`; CI 10/10; built and INSTALLED (DMG sha256 `45f92f20…`, health 200, 134 quotes intact). **This change ships no new string literal**, so the marker is the affected chunk's content hash — `StandardCalc-D_Yx5n6O.js` → `StandardCalc-Chq2SOkh.js`, with the installed file sha256-compared against the build output rather than grepping for a string that would read non-zero either way (Lesson 43). Checked before handing it over: **0 saved quotes carry a `pin_metric`**, so no existing quote moves its price on open.
>
> **Sprint S-MARGIN-HOLD — tick the margin at the header and the price follows it, 2026-09-18 (closing SHA: `d3b8422` via PR #368).** S-MARGIN-PIN the day before made the pin **remember and warn**; this makes it **drive**. Henry's direction: _"thêm ô tick vào sau Header VA%, Contr%, GM% … nếu chọn tick box Contr% thì giá điều chỉnh theo thay đổi process giữ nguyên Contr%, không tick được cả 3 ô"_, then _"tick ở header, duyệt xong thì tự nhả ghim"_. The solver was never the missing piece — `priceSolver` has solved for a target margin since before this branch, and the margin cells have been editable for as long. What did not exist was **holding** one: move a process rate and the price sits still while the margin slides off the number somebody set. **The metric is quote-level, the held value is per tier, and that split is the load-bearing decision.** A header tick spans every column-row, so it cannot mean different metrics on different tiers; but each tier has its own price and its own costs, so the number it holds has to be its own. `readTierPin` composes the two and returns `null` unless BOTH halves are present — which is what makes a half-written pin left by the previous cut read as _nothing pinned_ rather than as a pin on an unknown metric, pinned by its own test. **Exactly one metric is holdable** because two of the three cannot be satisfied at once — two equations, one unknown — so the other two cells go read-only rather than silently losing an argument with the solver. **One widening of the instruction, stated rather than buried**: Henry said release on approval; the gate ships as **draft-only**, which also stops the auto-drive at `quote_to_sale`, before `price_approved`. The reasoning is that a quote under sales review is being read at a specific number just as much as an approved one, and a price that moves under a reviewer is the hazard the release exists to close. It is one line and Henry can narrow it back. The tick stays visible and explains itself out of draft instead of vanishing, so nobody wonders where the feature went. **The one piece that could genuinely misbehave is extracted and tested for that reason**: an effect that writes state it also reads spins forever unless the write settles. It settles in one pass normally — re-solving lands the metric on target, the next pass sees no drift — but a cent-scale price whose one 4-decimal step moves the margin more than the tolerance never would. `planAutoHold` refuses to write the **same price twice running**, which breaks that cycle without having to predict which tiers are at risk, and four tests drive the guard directly. **Tolerance is `0.0005`, half the cells' own 0.1pp display step** — price rounds to 4 decimals so an achieved percentage never equals a pinned one exactly, and a tolerance any tighter would report every held tier as drifted forever. `toggleHold` snapshots the **canonical** `result[metric]`, never the what-if-masked display value, or ticking during a what-if would pin a number the quote does not actually have. Cpx routes through `SET_CPLX_FIELD` / `SET_CPLX_EXTRA_MOQ` per **MES-3-FIX-53** — the Std action lands writes in `stdState` and loses them on save — with a test on the routing itself. **Two of my own process errors, recorded**: the VA/CONTR/GM header trio appears **twice** in each tab (Selling and Target), so two patch attempts anchored on the wrong copy before I printed the file verbatim and replaced only the first; and `MarginPriceCells.helpers.js` was imported without its `.js` extension, which Vite tolerates and `node --test` does not — which is precisely why that file had never been tested. **Verify**: client **2062 → 2066 pass**, 0 fail; ESLint **0 errors / 314 warnings measured against `main` in a clean worktree, identical**; prettier clean; CI 10/10. Built and **INSTALLED** per Lesson 37 (DMG sha256 `e613ce2c…`, health 200, 134 quotes intact). **The marker was chosen the Lesson-43 way and the first two candidates were rejected by it**: `pin_metric` and `pin_pct` already read **1** in the installed bundle because PR #366 shipped that API the day before — a false non-zero would have reported the change as already live. `mpc-hold` measured **0 installed / 2 fresh**, and reads **2** in the installed bundle after. **Correction, same day**: what this entry describes as shipped did not actually drive the price — the driver was placed inside the Cost Breakdown tab, which is unmounted while costs are edited. The entry is left as written because the reasoning it records is still the design; **`aba60ab` (PR #370, Sprint S-MARGIN-HOLD-FIX above) is where it became true.** Rewriting it to read correctly would hide that the first cut shipped inert, which is the part worth keeping.
>
> **Sprint S-MARGIN-PIN — the margin target you typed was solved from and then forgotten, 2026-09-18 (closing SHA: `aafbd3c` via PR #366).** Henry's report: _"sửa cont là 30%, sau đó điều chỉnh một cái gì đó trên bảng tính thì giá nó không đổi mà Contr % lại thay đổi"_ — he wanted a way to pin the price to a margin instead of the other way round. **Two options were written up before any code, and the report was wrong in a way worth recording.** It proposed building a price solver; `client/src/services/priceSolver.js` **already had one**, wired to editable VA/Contr/GM cells in Cost Breakdown that back-solve the price per tier for any of the three metrics. Typing 30 into Contr% has worked for a while. The report missed it because the search went `calcEngine` → display tabs and never grepped for _solve_ or _target_; the same audit-first lesson as #196 one day earlier, missed again. **What was genuinely absent is the memory**: the 30% was never stored, so the next cost edit moved the metric while the price stood still and nothing said so. The existing warning fires on the **company floor** (`FLOORS` = GM/Contr 25%, VA 30%), and a pin of 30% sliding to 27% is still above the floor — so the case that hurts is exactly the case that stayed silent. Correcting the report shrank the job to about a quarter, which is the argument for reporting before coding rather than after. **The three pieces shipped**: the pin `{metric, pct}` persists per tier (`readTierPin` / `planTierPinWrite`, routing tier-0-vs-extra and std-vs-cpx through the same shape as `planTierPriceWrite` so the two cannot drift); the cell turns amber with the gap spelled out (`Now 27.3% — 2.7% below the 30.0% you pinned`); one click on ↻ re-solves at the pinned target, **through `commitMetric`** so the price is written by the same path that set it rather than a second one that could disagree. **Three decisions that are judgement, not mechanics.** _Amber, not the red the floor uses_ — drifting off your own pin needs a look, breaching the floor must not ship, and one colour for two meanings drains the red of meaning. _Selling side only_ — Target is the customer's number (#345), and you cannot hold someone else's price to your own margin, so a pin there would promise something meaningless; typing a % there still solves, it just records nothing. _Tolerance = half the cells' display granularity (0.05pp)_ — price rounds to 4 decimals so the achieved % is never exactly the pin, and flagging a gap that still renders as `30.0%` is crying wolf, which is how people learn to ignore the warning that matters (Lesson 0's shape, one surface over). **A pre-existing blocker found on the way**: `MarginPriceCells.helpers.js` imported `priceSolver` **without the `.js` extension**, which Vite tolerates and `node --test` does not — which is why that file had never carried a unit test. The repo already runs two conventions, extension-less for app code (633 imports) and explicit for files that are tested (`CalcLeadTimeNotice.helpers.js`); adding the extension moved this file into the second group rather than inventing a third. **Tests**: 17 new pure tests, client **2042 → 2059**. One pins the **MES-3-FIX-53** trap — Complex must route through `SET_CPLX_*`, and sending it through the Std action lands the write in `stdState` and loses it on save; sabotage-checking that branch reddens exactly one test and no other. Others cover the dirty shapes: a half-written pin (metric without pct) reads as no pin rather than guessing, an unknown metric name is refused so a future rename cannot silently drive the UI, and a legacy quote with no pin reads `null` instead of throwing. **Std and Cpx were wired in the same PR on purpose** — they are twins, and shipping one and scheduling the other is precisely how they drift (MES-3-FIX-40, MES-3-FIX-56). The non-drifted cell still renders the bare `<input>` with no wrapper, because these sit in a dense grid and the common case must not grow an element. **Verify**: ESLint 0 errors with 353 warnings identical to `main`, prettier clean, perf budget green, CI 10/10; built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37, marker `mpc-pin-btn` 0 → 2 files in the installed bundle, measured against the installed bundle first per Lesson 43; 134 quotes and `integrity_check ok` after the swap. **Operational the same day, no PR — the tooling-cost formula audited end to end against RFQ-2026-S0061** (quote #200). The chain in `calcProcess` is `EAU = eau_ovr || (annual_qty || moq) × (product_lifetime || 1)`, `cap = EAU × 0.8`, denominator `= MIN(tool_life × layout, cap)` with **Jig/Jig&Fixture not multiplying by layout** (a jig holds the part, it does not wear per shot × cavity). Verified not by hand-arithmetic but against `result.rows.processes[]` **as the app itself stored it**: 0.04125 + 0.00675 + 0.0050625 = **0.0530625**, exact. Three findings worth carrying. (1) **The EAU cap binds on every row here** — `20000 × 3 = 60000` against a cap of `16000` — so at this quote's values editing Tool Life or Layout changes the cost by nothing at all until Tool Life drops below 5,334; only tool price, EAU and the 0.8 factor move the number. (2) **Two of the three tool costs are hand-typed and untraceable**: `cutter_costs: ['108','81']` — the app has a cutter-cost formula but an operator-entered value overrides it, so there is no record of how 108 was arrived at. Only the $660 plate is derived (`Silk screen` base `$110` × 6 colours, from `ddl_sites.json → VN.plate_base_cost`). (3) **A row's `tool_cost` reading 0 does not mean the tooling is free** — all three rows carry `tool_cost_src` pointing at Layout (`plate`, `cutter-0`, `cutter-1`), and when that is set the row's own Tool Cost cell is ignored entirely. **And one dead branch found while answering "what happens if EAU is blank"**: it falls back to **MOQ**, which for this quote would amortise over 800 instead of 16,000 and inflate tooling **20×** — the "$237.50 tools" report of 2026-06-12 was exactly this. The gate from #311 now makes EAU one of four fields the RFQ tab will not release, so the path is unreachable in normal use; but the specific tooltip S-INPUT-WARN wrote for it (`moqcard.eau_required`, explaining the tooling consequence) is now **unreachable code** — the gate's generic `gate.required_tip` is checked first and always wins. Reported, not fixed.
>
> **Sprint S-CHAT-RECALL-POLICY — the two divergences #362 refused to resolve quietly, ruled on and shipped, 2026-09-17 (closing SHA: `85b45ff` via PR #364).** PR #362 built the Messages-tab recall button against the semantics the server already had (own-only, 15-minute window) and wrote the two gaps against issue #196's recommendation up for Henry rather than resolving them inside a UI PR. He ruled: **admin override, and no time limit.** This is that ruling, as its own PR — which is the point of having surfaced it rather than guessing. **Recall loses its window entirely**: a message worth taking back is usually one noticed hours later, so a 15-minute rule mostly denies the request at the moment it is actually made. **Admin override is scoped to rooms the admin belongs to, and that scoping is the load-bearing decision.** `GET /rooms/:id/messages` already answers **403 to a non-member**, so an admin outside a room cannot read its messages on any screen; without the membership half, a bare message id in a `curl` would delete a **private DM the caller cannot even open**, and ids are sequential so they are guessable. The constraint therefore takes nothing from an admin that they could already see — moderating a room means joining it, and joining is itself visible — while closing an enumeration hole that "admin override" read literally would have opened. A test drives exactly that: admin DELETEs into a DM between two other users → **403, `deleted_at` still null**. **What deliberately did NOT change, and why it is not timidity**: `editMessage` and `purgeMessage` keep their 15-minute windows, and purge stays **author-only with no override**. Editing silently rewrites history, purging destroys the row, recall leaves a tombstone everybody can see — three different consequences should not share one rule, and only recall was ruled on. **Two regression guards pin the distinction** so a later tidy-up pass cannot quietly "make it consistent": one asserts edit still answers `window_expired`, the other that purge refuses an admin AND still expires. **The `deleted_by` column is what makes the override honest.** Added through the same idempotent `COLUMN_ADDS` path `delivered_at` used, additive and nullable, so existing tombstones read null and render exactly as before. Without it an admin override means an operator's message vanishes with no way to tell whether an admin removed it or they fumbled it themselves — the spooky version of the feature rather than the moderation one. The tombstone now reads `(removed by an administrator)` in that case; it stays English like the rest of that untranslated feed, while the button and confirm dialog use `t()` as chrome, which is the same split #362 settled on. **Every recall is audited now**, the override under its own event name (`CHAT_MESSAGE_DELETE_OVERRIDE` vs `CHAT_MESSAGE_DELETE`) with JSON detail per MES-3-FIX-3 — **both** cases logged, deliberately, so the two can never be told apart by the _absence_ of a record. **Tests**: server **1205 → 1216 pass** / 1 chronic skip, client **2042**. Three tests were written first and **watched failing for the right reason** — checked that the failures were real assertions (`actual: false, expected: true`) and not a missing import, which is the difference between a red test and a broken test file. The admin branch was then sabotage-checked: turning `return opts.isAdmin === true` into `return false` reddens **exactly one** test and no other. **Two of my own errors, recorded rather than smoothed over**: `getDb` was imported from `chatStore.js`, which does not export it (it lives in `db/connection.js`) — the test failed with `getDb is not a function`, a failure for the wrong reason that would have looked like a product bug if I had read only the count; and seeding a fourth user for the admin case turned the directory test's `users.length === 3` red, which is a **fixture** change, so the number was updated **with the reason written beside it** rather than silently bumped. **Operational note**: the first launch after install runs `ALTER TABLE chat_messages ADD COLUMN deleted_by` against the live `ops.db`. Additive and on the path `delivered_at` already took, but it is still a schema change on production data and was called out to Henry rather than left to be discovered.
>
> **Sprint S-MESSAGES-RECALL — the delete feature that was four-fifths already built, 2026-09-17 (closing SHA: `b62f395` via PR #362, closing issue #196).** The issue was filed 2026-06-21 during the engineering freeze with a full server-and-client scope, and by the time its two gates opened (freeze closed 2026-06-26, hypercare ended ~2026-09-13) **most of what it specified had shipped without the issue being updated**. Auditing before writing (Lesson 7) found `DELETE /api/chat/messages/:id` doing soft delete AND `?hard=1` purge at `chat.js:335`, the SSE fan-out, integration tests for the 403-non-author and 410-double-delete cases, and the whole flow live in the floating drawer. **The actual gap was one-directional**: the Messages tab RENDERS tombstones (`Bubble` at line 374) and shows `(deleted)` in the conversation list, so it reacts to deletions made elsewhere — it simply offered no way to start one, and an operator who sent a message from the tab had to open the drawer to take it back. So this PR adds an affordance and touches no server code. **The four open questions the issue left, three of which the shipped code had already answered**: the issue recommended own + admin override, anytime, tombstone, all surfaces. The server does own-only (no admin override), a **15-minute window** (`EDIT_WINDOW_MS`), tombstone, all surfaces. The UI follows **what is shipped**, not what the issue recommended, because changing a live recall window or adding cross-user delete rights is a security and product decision that has no business riding inside a UI-parity PR — both divergences are written up on the PR for Henry to rule on rather than silently resolved. The same reasoning kept the purge button off the tab: server-side `?hard=1` is **author + window**, NOT the sys-only the issue imagined, so surfacing it would have spread a semantics mismatch to a second screen. **The gating rule was extracted to `messageActions.js` rather than inlined**, for the reason this repo always extracts: `node:test` with no React testing infra means logic inside JSX cannot be asserted at all. One of its 11 tests pins a trap worth keeping — **`Number(null)` is `0`**, so without an explicit null check a message with no author and a viewer with no id compare equal and a stranger is handed a delete button; deleting that one guard line turns exactly that test red and no other, which is the only evidence that a guard is guarding. **A defect found by tracing the new path rather than by a test**: the SSE `message_deleted` handler only ever wrote to `messages`, so after a recall the bubble read "(message deleted)" while the sidebar beside it still showed the original text until the next full refresh — `ConversationRow` has always carried the tombstone branch and simply never received the update. It reproduces today by deleting from the drawer with the tab open, so it is pre-existing, but the new button makes it reachable in one click on the very screen where the sidebar is visible. Fixed for soft delete; **purge deliberately left alone**, because the correct preview there is the PREVIOUS message, which the client does not hold — that needs a refetch, not a local patch, and saying so beats a wrong-looking half-fix. **Two things deliberately NOT done.** The CSS anchors on `.msg-bubble`, which already declares `position: relative` 40 lines earlier; the duplicate declaration written first was removed and replaced by a comment naming the dependency, because a second `position` on one selector later in the same file is exactly how PR #315 silently un-stuck a sticky header. And the tab's tombstone stayed English: the whole Messages tab is untranslated (`No messages yet`, `You: `, `Inbox`), so translating two strings inside an otherwise-English feed produces a half-Vietnamese screen that reads as broken rather than as translated — the new strings reuse the existing `chat.recall` / `chat.recall_confirm` / `chat.edit_cancel` keys (no new keys added, all already EN+VI), and the tab's own i18n belongs in its own wave. **Verify**: client suite 2031 → **2042**, ESLint 0 errors with the 3 inline-style warnings on this file unchanged from `main` (none added), prettier clean, perf budget green, CI 10/10; built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with `msg-recall-btn` grepped in the installed bundle 0 → 1 before and after — the candidate marker was measured against the installed bundle FIRST per Lesson 43, which ruled out `chat.recall_confirm` since the drawer already ships it.

> **Sprint S-EXPORT-SCN5 — the HMAC verify pre-flight finally run on prod, closing issue #57 after four months, 2026-09-17 (no PR; measured against installed `7903478` on `main` at `5c5444c`).** The issue had been deferred since 2026-05-20 for three reasons, and **all three had gone stale without anyone noticing**: "no SSH access to prod server" (prod is THIS machine — `/Applications/Ops Control.app`, LaunchAgent `vn.ccldesign.opscontrol.server`, `/health :3100` = 200), "no engineer available to run verify.js" (`server/services/quoteExport/verify.js` has been in the tree since MVP-2), and a missing key (it is in `<userData>/.env`, 64 hex, valid). A deferral whose reasons expire silently stays deferred forever; MVP-3 re-import is the thing that will refuse unsigned files, and it has not shipped, so this was the last quiet moment to do it. **The result: HMAC-FP `9edaa455`, identical to the fingerprint recorded when the key was seeded on 2026-05-20 — the prod key has not drifted in four months**, so the recovery playbook was not needed. **The part that makes that measurement worth anything**: the fingerprint was taken from the environment of the RUNNING server process (the PID listening on 3100), not only from `.env`. Reading the key out of `.env` and then verifying a file signed with that same key proves nothing — it is `.env` agreeing with itself; what SCN5 asks is whether the process actually serving quotes holds the key you think it does.
