Round-trip on prod quote #198 (RFQ-2026-S0059, internal variant): verify with the real key **OK** (`schemaSha 2ab0c53f`, 116 state keys decoded, key set identical to the source quote); with a random 64-hex key **FAIL**, as step 4 requires. Three further tamper shapes beyond the spec all failed correctly — a mutated payload chunk (`_Schema!B1`) caught by the manifest sha256, a mutated HMAC cell (`A2`), and a removed `_Audit` sheet. **The control that makes those three mean something**: load the clean workbook through ExcelJS and save it again with no edit at all — it still verifies, so the failures are caused by the mutations and not by re-serialisation. Without that line all three "FAIL" results would have been unfalsifiable. **A mistake recorded rather than quietly fixed**: the first tamper attempt mutated `_Schema!A3` and reported NOT DETECTED, which looked like a P0 in the signing chain. It was my test. Chunks live in **row 1 starting at column B** (`FIRST_CHUNK_COL = 2`, with `A1` the manifest and `A2` the HMAC), so `A3` is an empty cell no reader touches — nothing signed had changed and verify was right to pass. Checking the layout before believing the alarm cost one command; reporting it would have cost a day. **Deliverable**: `4. CLAUDE OUTPUT/uat-2026-09-17/golden.xlsx` (sha256 `526c5a198fd86173338c8284975fde0cdf386c58a04982278a1256ec8c7b6eb0`) with a README carrying the provenance, per R3 mitigation #3 — a file signed by the real prod key for MVP-3 re-import to be tested against. That directory lives OUTSIDE the repo (`Claude-Cowork/4. CLAUDE OUTPUT` vs the repo at `Claude-Cowork/3. PROJECT/Ops-Control`) and outside the off-site mirror's scope, which matters because an internal-variant export carries the customer's name and the margin. **Stated limitation rather than glossed**: the golden was produced by calling `exportQuote()` directly, so `engine_sha` and `library_fingerprint` on `_Audit` read `-` (the HTTP route supplies those and no session was authenticated). The HMAC signs the `_Schema` payload, NOT the `_Audit` sheet, so the signature chain is unaffected — but if a route-produced golden is ever wanted, exporting once from the UI replaces the file.
>
> **Sprint S-LEADTIME-EXPORT — what the Lead time tab promises, and what the export actually carried, 2026-09-16 (closing SHA: `7903478` via PR #354).** Five PRs, all five started by Henry reading a file the app produced or looking at the screen that builds one. **#348 `b2c083f` — PO L/T quoted machine time and called it a lead time.** The rule was `ceil(Σ PROD TIME / 8)`, which for the 4.76 production hours of a typical label job answered **"1 days"**. That is how long the presses run, not how long the customer waits: it says nothing about queue, material on hand, or the PO sitting in purchasing, so it was never a date anyone could commit to. The rule Henry specified quotes the commercial window instead — at or under **100 production hours** a flat `7~14 working days`, and above it every **8-hour block** pushes BOTH ends out one day (`8~15`, `9~16`, …). The block count is a **ceiling**, so passing the threshold at all already costs the first day: 100.1 h reads `8~15`, while exactly 100 h stays inside the flat band. The gap between the two ends is always 7 days, pinned at five different sizes. **The rename is the load-bearing part**: `derivePoLeadTime` returned DAYS and the new rule needs the raw HOURS to compare against the threshold, so rather than quietly change what one name returns it became `derivePoProdHours` (hours or null) plus a separate `formatPoLeadTime` — which makes all seven call sites **fail to resolve** instead of silently receiving hours where they expected days. `poLtAuto` followed for the same reason; a variable that now holds hours must not keep a name that says days (Lesson 21). **No production time still yields an empty cell**, never a quoted window — a quote with no processes has no lead time to promise — and the manual `lt_po_ovr` override still wins. The Legend's formula note was rewritten in both languages because it described a divide-by-8 rule that no longer exists. Shipped first as `7~ 14`, corrected to `7~14` on Henry's confirmation. One correction of mine: a test asserted 120 h → `9~16`, but `ceil((120-100)/8)` is `ceil(2.5)` = **3** blocks, so `10~17` — the helper was right and the expectation was wrong. **#349 `f5340f5` — the REMARK on screen and the REMARK in the file were built by different functions.** The shared Lead time & Notice component renders `buildRemarkBlock(rows, selection, product_tolerance)` — a `1. Clear materials MOQ.` header, one bullet per checked row, and a `2. Product tolerance: +/- <n>mm` footer. Both save paths called `buildRemarkFromSelection`, which is **literally the middle third of that same function**, so what persisted was a strict subset of what the operator was looking at, and Cost Breakdown, its CSV and the xlsx all showed the fragment. Measured before fixing: of the **37** saved quotes carrying a remark, **37 were missing both lines and 0 were complete** — quote 197 (`RFQ-2026-S0050`) held `- BW-7513: —` with `product_tolerance: 0.2` sitting unused right beside it, which is the exact cell Henry exported. **Passing the tolerance is part of the fix, not decoration**: `buildRemarkBlock` falls back to a default for a missing third argument, so calling it without one would have persisted the default on every quote regardless of what was typed — the lint guard asserts the argument, not just the function name. **The second half only became visible once the first was fixed**: Summarize ran the remark through `toBulletFromTextarea`, which prefixes `• ` to every line, so a saved block would have rendered `• 1. Clear materials MOQ.` — a bullet on a heading. The remark already carries its own structure and is now passed through verbatim; PROCESS and TYPE OF MATERIAL keep their bullets because they are plain operator notes with none. Excel safety is unaffected: `csvEscape` (#317) still neutralises a cell leading with `-`, which is what legacy rows still start with. With no React test infrastructure in this repo the wiring is pinned at the **source level** (`remarkBuilder.lint.test.js`), the same approach as `libraryRefresh.lint.test.js` and `deadCode.lint.test.js`: every save path must use the builder the screen uses, none may call the bullets-only one, and the call must pass `product_tolerance` — three of the four assertions watched failing first. **Forward-fix only, deliberately**: the 37 quotes keep their truncated text until re-saved, because re-deriving a remark for a quote whose material rows or checkbox selection have since changed would invent text nobody approved. **#350 `fc974c0` — a column that reached no export at all.** Henry marked an empty `CCL PN (80#)` column on an exported file, wedged between RFQ NO and Direct Customer. It was empty because it did not exist: neither `SUMMARIZE_COLUMNS` nor the row builder had `ccl_pn`, while **110 of the 133** live quotes carry one. Added immediately after `rfq_no` so the CSV reads `… | Sale Owner | RFQ NO | CCL PN (80#) | Direct Customer | …`, exactly where he put it; the label drops the form's trailing `*`, which marks the field required on the RFQ card and is not part of its name. **Checked for a Lesson-21 duplicate before shipping**, because the export already showed the same string under Direct CU PN and End CU PN — it is not one: quote 197's saved state carries `LABEL ETC-MAIN OQC  YELLOW 25x25mm` independently in `ccl_pn`, `direct_cu_pn` AND `end_cu_pn`, so the operator typed it three times and the export was reporting faithfully. The 38-key shape guard fired as designed and was bumped to **39 with the reason written into the column-add history**, not edited silently — the handling #319 established. **#352 `da3b5fa` — the selection you cannot see.** Selecting rows is how an operator builds an export, and the table runs to 138 rows, so a tick three screens down is invisible: the header read `3 ROW(S) SELECTED` while two were on screen and there was nothing to check the third against before the file was written. Ticked rows now float to the top and each new pick queues behind the previous ones; selection order comes from the `Set`'s own iteration order, so un-ticking and re-ticking sends a quote to the BACK of the queue, which is what the operator sees happen. **Grouped by QUOTE, not by row**, and that is the part that needed thinking about: `rowsForExport` already expands a ticked tier to every tier of its quote (#317), so floating only the ticked row would put two of a quote's three tiers on top and leave the third below **while the file gets all three** — the screen would be lying about the export. The table and the export now read the same ordered list, pinned by a test that runs both together. An empty selection returns the **same array reference** (no selection, no reshuffle, no re-render), and a ticked id hidden by the search box — which stays in `selected` by design — floats nothing rather than throwing. **#354 `7903478` — the float exposed an older inconsistency, and two reports came back.** Five ticks floated SEVEN rows with two of them un-ticked in the middle of the selection, and Henry read that as rows the float had missed. It was not. Checked against live data: screen rows 1–2 are quote **#27** (`saved_at 2026-06-10T14:07Z`, RFQ blank so the row shows the `Q<id>` fallback) and rows 3–4 are quote **#104** (`2026-04-03T07:46Z`), **both 2-tier** — 2+2+1+1+1 is exactly the seven rows on screen. Those two were the sibling tiers of quotes that WERE ticked, and `rowsForExport` has expanded a ticked tier to the whole quote since #317, so pushing them down would have made the screen lie about the file. **The real defect predates #352**: the checkbox had per-ROW semantics while the export had per-QUOTE semantics, and floating the selection is simply what made the mismatch visible — five ticks, seven exported rows, and no way to see why. A tick now selects the QUOTE, so no un-ticked row can appear among the selection and the counter equals the export's row count (7, not 5). Resolved against the **full pre-filter row set**, because ticking only the visible tiers would leave an un-ticked sibling to appear the moment the filter is cleared, on a quote the operator believes is whole; select-all follows the same rule so the header box and the row boxes cannot disagree. **The second report was the same surface, opposite direction**: searching again dropped the earlier picks off screen and the header read `NO ROWS SELECTED` over a table whose selection was still alive in memory — an operator builds an export by searching one RFQ, ticking it, searching the next, so the basket has to survive. Ticked rows are now pinned on screen regardless of the filter, pinned first and floated second so the basket reads in tick order. The `(N more hidden by filter)` hint went with it: pinning makes that branch unreachable, and text that can never render is worse than no text — the counter was repointed from `sorted` to `shown` for the same reason. The reported case is now a test: five ticks across those five quotes yield seven ticked rows, zero un-ticked rows on top, and an export identical to the screen. **The verify-marker trap, twice in one day and in two different shapes** (see Lesson 43): first `"VND"` read 0 in the MaterialLibrary chunk after a correct install because rolldown had hoisted `materialCurrency.js` into its own chunk; then `CCL PN (80#)` and `Clear materials MOQ` read NON-zero in the pre-install bundle, because the first is the RFQ form's field label and the second a constant in the helpers — both present regardless of the patch. Real markers were found by scoping to the specific chunk (`ccl_pn` in `Summarize-*.js`, 0 → 3) and by diffing the minified text (`remark:x(e.lt_remark)` → `remark:e.lt_remark`, and `product_tolerance` 0 → 1 in both calculator chunks). On #352 the lesson was applied rather than relearned: `orderBySelection` is minified away, so three candidates were measured against the installed bundle FIRST, two came back 0/0 — proving nothing either way — and only `.flat()` (0 → 1) and `new Map(` (1 → 2), both scoped to the Summarize chunk, were used. **Tests**: client **2002 → 2031 pass**, server 1205 pass / 1 chronic skip; ESLint 0 errors, prettier clean; CI 10/10 on all three. Every PR merged, built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37. **Operational, no PR — three live-data repairs, 2026-09-17.** All three are the data half of fixes that shipped the day before; each one was inspected READ-ONLY first, then applied with the server stopped, both stores backed up, and every store the read path consumes updated (Lesson 34) — `ops.db` `state_json` AND `raw_json`, plus the JSON file — then each verified separately and the server restarted. No server module was ever loaded against the live dir (Lesson 33); the edits are plain `sqlite3` + JSON. **(1) The REMARK back-fill — and a correction to what #349 claimed.** That PR said a back-fill was impossible because re-deriving a remark for a quote whose material rows had since changed would invent text nobody approved. **That reasoning was wrong**, and the wrongness is specific: the two missing lines need NO library lookup at all — the header is a constant and the tolerance is already stored on the quote, while the bullets are already stored too. So completing the text is a string join over data the quote already carries, and it is strictly MORE conservative than a UI re-save, which would refresh the bullets against today's library. Before running it, the composition was checked to reproduce `buildRemarkBlock` byte-for-byte on a fixture, and the constants were imported from the app's own module rather than retyped. Measured first: **0 of the 37 carried a manual `lt_remark_ovr`**, so nothing an operator typed was at risk; 36 were completed (the 37th already had the header). All three stores then read **37 complete / 0 truncated / 96 empty** — the 96 being quotes with no remark at all, deliberately untouched. Backups at `ops.db.before-remark-backfill-*` / `quote_history.json.before-remark-backfill-*`. **(2) One inherited Target cleared.** Quote **#199** (`RFQ-2026-S0057`) still held `target 0.1557 / 4062đ` inherited from #198 before #345 landed; `target` and `target_vnd` set to `null` (what the state factories use for unset), with the sell price, its VND mirror and the USD rate untouched. **(3) Three material dates typed as 2029.** `AW0450P`, `3M SUPER 20 TAPE` and `3M SUPER 20 TAPE (W8)` read `2029-06-18`; Henry confirmed 2024, so they are now `2024-06-18` in `npi_materials.json` **and** in the shadow `materials` table (the `date` column and `raw_json` both). 2029 was the only year above 2026 in the whole 3061-row library, and the rows surfaced the moment #346's sort shipped — the feature finding a data-entry error on its first use. **A sign the fix works through the UI too**: quote #102 (`RFQ-2026-S0004`) gained a complete four-line remark on its own at 00:52 through an ordinary Save, before any back-fill ran. **Also this day, no code**: the two 9-week-old stashes were triaged and dropped (patches archived outside the repo with the reasons) — `stash@{1}` held an independent WIP of **MES-3-FIX-61** that PR #242 (`03945d8`) had superseded nine days later, which is how that ticket was found still marked OPEN in this file and closed by #356 (`9e956e5`); `stash@{0}` was a stray deletion of `START_SERVER.command`, a launcher `deploy.sh:48` still points at. **And the off-site mirror was found to have never run unattended at this destination** — see Lesson 44. Its log showed five successes, all at human-typed times, against five scheduled failures. A Login Item runner was tried and reported one success at 09:28:16 which **did not repeat**, so it is stopped and the LaunchAgent stays disabled; the mirror runs manually on request until the cause is understood. The session explanation first recorded here was wrong and is corrected in Lesson 44.
>
> **Sprint S-MATLIB-CURRENCY — the price column learns which currency it is in, end to end, plus two defects operating the app turned up, 2026-09-16 (closing SHA: `ed4343a` via PR #346).** Four PRs, every one of them started by Henry using the installed app. **#343 `85be207` — a price column that asserted USD, and the hand-conversions that cost.** The NPI header read `USD / M²`, so the number was USD by assertion and anything a supplier quoted in đồng had to be divided by hand before it could be typed. The live library shows the result: one row's price field holds the literal string `6900 vnđ / roll` — `Number()` turns that into NaN, so it prices at **$0** in a quote — and another was entered as `2.45` with `giá 64000d/m2` written beside it, a hand division by 26122 that nobody can audit afterwards. The header now reads `PRICE` over `m²` and a **Currency** column sits beside it, a drop-down that can only be USD or VND; New Materials gained the same field. Conversion happens **once, at pick time**, in Materials & Process, against that quote's own `usd_rate` — `calcEngine` is untouched and stays currency-blind on purpose, and the NPI row is never rewritten, so choosing a VND material in one quote changes nothing for the next. Every one of the 3058 existing codes reads USD, which is exactly what the old header promised. **`priceInUsd` refuses rather than defaulting**, and the blank check sits BEFORE `Number()` because **`Number('')` is `0`, not `NaN`** — an empty price would otherwise sail through as a perfectly valid $0, which is the silent zero the helper exists to stop; 403 live rows are blank and 61 of the 132 quotes carry no usable USD rate (50 absent, 11 zero), so the refusing path is the common one, not the edge case. **#344 `5119e42` — the screen had the column, the file did not.** Henry exported and found 12 columns and no Currency. Export and import read **one** definition, `NPI_DATASET`, and it still declared the pre-#343 shape: the download wrote 12 headers with `price` still labelled `Price (USD/m²)`, and a file that DID carry a Currency column had it **silently dropped** at import — the Lesson-32 shape again. The dataset now declares 13 columns with `Currency` after `Price`, the label drops its USD assertion to match the screen (`Price (/m²)`, with the old spelling kept as an alias so files already on disk still import), and the aliases cover `Currency` / `CURRENCY` / `Currency (USD/VND)` / `Tiền tệ` — `usd` and `vnd` were already noise tokens in the tolerant matcher, so `Currency (USD/VND)` reduces to `{currency}` on its own. Two small pieces carry the rest. A new **`enum` coercion** (`coerceEnum` + `columnEnums`) makes a third currency an **issue in the wizard's Issues panel** instead of a value stored verbatim and then read back as USD by `normalizeCurrency` — a cell reading `VNĐ` on a 64000 price would display USD and quote **$64000**, invisible until someone reads the margin; blank still imports and means USD, because 3060 rows predate the column. And **`exportCell` + `exportDefaults`** make those 3060 rows export as `USD` rather than blank, so the file matches the screen instead of reading as "no data". **The guard that was guarding the wrong thing** is why an earlier attempt at this change was reverted instead of shipped: `mapHeaders: real CCL NPI export — ALL 12 columns map` asserted that **every canonical key** appears in a fixture captured BEFORE the currency column existed, so adding a column made it refuse a legacy file **for being legacy**. It now checks that fixture's own 12 headers map and that `currency` is legitimately absent, and a new test pins the CURRENT export header row round-tripping all 13 — see Lesson 42. **Left alone deliberately**: `POST /api/import/npi-materials` keeps its own exact-match `NPI_HEADER_MAP`; it has zero client callers (`costApi.uploadNpiMaterials` is defined in `api.js` and invoked nowhere) and Material Cost imports through the wizard, so widening a route nothing calls was not the ask. **#345 `d730312` — the Target price box filled itself.** Nothing computes that number; it is simply **never cleared**, so it rides from one quote to the next and the operator meets it already sitting in the box. Found in live data, not by a test: quote **199** (`RFQ-2026-S0057`, option OP1) was copied from quote **198** (`RFQ-2026-S0059`, OP2) nineteen minutes earlier — **93 of 99** scalar fields identical, and exactly six differ (rfq_number, options, ccl_pn, the schema stamp, and the sell price the operator retyped, 0.1549 → 0.1129). The Target came along untouched, **0.1557 / 4062đ on both rows**, now attached to an RFQ whose customer never gave that number. The rule written into the code: **Target is the customer's number for one RFQ, selling price is ours** — so it belongs to the RFQ, not the product, and a copy is by definition a different RFQ or a different option of one. The copy branch already kept a documented "Copy = fresh start" list (quote identity, pricing-snapshot provenance, process scrap); Target joins it, on the base tier and every extra MOQ tier, Std and Cpx. **Selling price is deliberately NOT cleared** — it carries over the same way, but the operator sets it on every quote and blanking it would empty the KPI strip and the whole Cost Breakdown on every copy; Henry confirmed both numbers are his to enter and to leave the PR as scoped. Opening the same quote still preserves the Target exactly, pinned by a test; the New-quote factories already started it `null`, so that path was clean. **Forward-fix only** — `RFQ-2026-S0057` still carries the inherited 0.1557 and has to be corrected by hand. **#346 `ed4343a` — sorting the Update Date column.** Clicking the header sorts newest-first, then toggles — the same first-click direction Quote History uses, so the two tables behave alike under the same gesture. The column is free text and the library shows it: of 3061 rows, **2116** are `YYYY-MM-DD`, **754** say `Old`, **91** say `DAP`, **15** are `7.2023` (month.year, no day), and a handful read `26.05.2025 (add 20% on 1.4.2026)` — a real update date followed by a pricing note, with **two rows that are ONLY the note**. `dateSortKey` is **anchored at the START of the cell**, and that one decision makes both of those come out right: the leading date wins over the note, and a cell that is only a note reads as having no update date instead of being filed among the newest. An unanchored "first date anywhere in the string" rule reads `(add 15% on 1.4.2026)` as 2026-04-01 and gets both wrong. The **857** undated rows stay together at the **bottom in BOTH directions**, in their existing order — floating them up on an ascending sort would bury the genuinely oldest entries under 850 rows of `Old`. Sorting runs **after the filter and before the page slice** so it orders the whole matching set rather than re-shuffling the 200 rows on screen, and the click resets to page 0; edits and deletes resolve their row by **object identity** (`data.indexOf(r)`), so reordering cannot mis-target them. The header is a real `<button>` inside the `<th>` — keyboard reachable and announced as a control — with `aria-sort` on the `th`. Sorting immediately surfaced three rows dated **2029-06-18** at the top of the newest-first view, almost certainly a typo; data left untouched and reported. **Three corrections worth keeping.** (1) The pre-recorded verify marker for #343 was `"VND"` in the MaterialLibrary chunk and it read **0 after a correct install** — `materialCurrency.js` is imported by three tabs, so rolldown hoisted it into **its own chunk**; the marker was wrong, not the build. A chunk-local marker is only valid for a module that lives in exactly one chunk. (2) `set -- $var` **does not word-split in zsh** — used a second time this session to tabulate SHAs against `CLAUDE.md`, and `grep -c ""` duly reported the file's whole line count as a match for every row; redone with `read`. (3) A test asserting `sortByDate([undefined, null])` round-trips was wrong, not the helper: `Array.prototype.sort` **hoists literal `undefined` elements to the end before the comparator ever runs**. **Tests**: client **1959 → 2002 pass**, server **1191 → 1205 pass** / 1 chronic skip (MES-3-FIX-51); ESLint 0 errors and prettier clean throughout; CI 10/10 on all four PRs. Every PR merged, built and **INSTALLED** per Lesson 37 with minify-proof markers grepped in the installed bundle before and after, and every new guard watched failing for the right reason first — removing the Currency alias turned 3 red, removing `exportDefaults` 1, removing `columnTypes.currency` 1, and the un-cleared Target 2.
>
> **Sprint S-MATLIB-SURFACE — four faults on the material-library surface, every one found by Henry operating the installed app, 2026-09-16 (closing SHA: `0c3497c` via PR #341).** Nothing here was found by a test. Each started as a screenshot of something that looked wrong. **#339 `db14fab` — Quote Materials carried the description, not the code.** The Cost Breakdown column and its CSV showed `PP (Synthetic Paper - remove glue) · 0.088 · White` where the operator wanted the `BW-7513` he reads in the grid. The trap is that **the column headed "IFS CODE" is bound to `mat.code`, NOT `mat.ifs_code`** — a Lesson-21 shape, where the label and the storage disagree. `ifs_code` turns out to be a **shadow field**: no input anywhere is bound to it, it is written only as a side effect of picking from the library, and across the 132 live quotes it is set on **4 material rows out of 656** and **never holds a value different from `code`**. Reading it alone would have blanked the column for 652 rows, so the resolution is `ifs_code || code` — reused verbatim from `03-materials.js:278`, which the xlsx Materials sheet already shipped, so the two exports cannot disagree about what an IFS code is. Measured before shipping, Main.Mat rows only: **204 change content, 0 go blank, 159 that were EMPTY become populated**. The on-screen column moves with the CSV because they are one column definition; `deriveInboxRow` also computes `quote_materials` but the Approvals inbox renders `drw_materials`, so that screen is untouched. **#340 `a595b29` — a new material was invisible to every calculator until the app restarted.** Henry added `KCW/RPS6/KDL`, saw it listed in Material Cost, then got **"0 results"** searching it in the pricing picker one tab away. `MaterialLibrary.jsx` keeps its OWN `useState` copy of `npiDB/ifsDB/sourcingDB`, fetches it itself and saves straight through `costApi.saveAll` — **it never touched `CostLibContext`**, which loads once on mount, has no SSE subscription, and receives no data event because save-all emits none for materials. So the context held its app-start snapshot and **every calculator in the app was stale, not just the picker**; the picker is merely where it showed. Timeline confirmed it to the minute: app installed 11:14, material saved 11:24, picker empty at 11:25. Three suspects were ruled out first — the NPI normalizer maps `code: row.name` so the row would map fine, `matches()` lowercases **both** sides so slashes and capitals were never involved, and the row really was in `npi_materials.json`. What named it an omission rather than a decision: **LibRate, LibDDL, LibFinance and InkCalculator all call `refreshLib()` after saving; Material Cost was the only library editor that did not.** Fixed with `refreshLib()` rather than pushing local state in via `setMaterials`, because the server normalises rows on save and the picker should show what was actually stored. Guarded by `libraryRefresh.lint.test.js` — any tab whose `saveAll` payload names a library dataset must reference a context refresher — deliberately **scoped to the library keys**, since SampleTracking / RFQTracker / QuoteHistory also call `saveAll` but persist tracking data no calculator reads, and a guard firing on those would cry wolf (Lesson 0). **#341 `0c3497c` — prefill the date, and the year filter that had never worked.** The add modal mounts with `row={{}}`, so Update Date opened blank and every entry was hand-typed in whatever shape the typist chose; the library shows the result — of 3059 NPI rows, **2115 are `YYYY-MM-DD`, 754 say literally "Old", 91 say "DAP"**. Prefilling alone would have been half the job, because the field feeds a filter that was **completely dead**: the dropdown was built from the LAST four characters (`String(r.date).slice(-4)`) while the filter matched the FIRST (`startsWith`), so `2026-09-16` yielded `"9-16"` as a "year". Measured on live data: **289 dropdown options and every single one filtered to ZERO rows.** Reading the real year gives **9** — 2026 → 710, 2025 → 534, 2024 → 512, 2023 → 437 — and the 852 rows carrying `Old`/`DAP`/blank correctly produce no option at all. `todayISO` builds from **local** date parts, never `toISOString()`: this box runs UTC+7 so a save before 07:00 would stamp yesterday, which is the fleet-table bug from #296 one field over, pinned by a midnight-local test. **A correction recorded rather than quietly fixed**: Sourcing was first reported as having the identical bug. It does not — it slices `(0, 4)`, which **agrees** with its own `startsWith`, and its filter returns real counts (2025 → 641 rows). It had been measured with NPI's formula. Its filter is untouched; only its blank-on-new date is prefilled, as `YYYY-MM`, the shape 1928 of its 2234 rows already use. See Lesson 41. **#338 `92e69c8` — repo housekeeping.** `_SALVAGE/` had sat untracked and un-gitignored at the repo root for months: 265 MB of electron build output for commit `c35d4a2` (which is in this repo's history, and three minor versions stale) deleted, and 116 KB of genuinely unique material kept — including `deploy/fleet-license/`, which closes a gap where **`CLAUDE.md` cited `mint-license.command` while the file existed nowhere in the repo**. Windows build docs kept with a STALE banner rather than presented as current (they describe v1.5.12), matching how the Apple design spec was archived. On the remote branches, the same tip-vs-merged-SHA test as 2026-09-15 matched **20 of 22** — and the two that did not (`feat/pricing-snapshot-phase-4`, `sprint/pending-approvals-cols-search`, each one commit beyond the merged SHA) were **KEPT**, because grepping main for their added lines was inconclusive after the i18n waves rewrote both files. A ref costs nothing; deleting work that cannot be proven landed does. **Operational, no PR**: the off-site mirror was run manually twice to carry the day's work off the disk ahead of its 4-hour cadence, and a stray **0-byte `ops.db` at the userData root** — created during the morning's off-site work, no sidecars, so never a real database — was removed with Henry's approval and swept from the share by `--delete`. It was found only because a path check of mine was **wrong** (`mirror/ops.db` instead of `mirror/data/ops.db`, with `awk` field numbers skewed by the space in "Application Support"); the mirror had been correct all along. **Verify**: every PR merged, built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with a minify-proof marker grepped in the installed bundle before and after — `ifs_code||….code` 0 → 1 file, `refreshLib` in the MaterialLibrary chunk 0 → 1, `slice(-4)` 1 → 0. Client suite 1959 → **1973 pass**; server 1191 pass / 1 chronic skip; ESLint 0 errors and prettier clean throughout; CI 10/10 on all four.
>
> **Sprint S-BACKUP-OFFSITE — retention that nothing obeyed, and an off-site mirror that had never once run, 2026-09-16 (closing SHA: `8824a32` via PR #336).** Three PRs from one audit thread. Each fix was found by measuring the live box, and the last one turned up a credential leak I created myself while fixing the others. **#334 `fb39dea` — the Library tarballs nothing ever deleted.** `getBackupRoot()` resolves to `Backup/**Data**`, so the cycle's prune step never reached `Backup/Library`, which the library step feeds a ~37 MB tarball every day. Live box: **93 files / 2.7 GB spanning 132 days**, while SQLite under the _same_ `OPS_BACKUP_RETENTION_DAYS` held exactly its 30-day window. The proof was already in the box's own last-run record and had been every night for months — `{"name":"prune","scanned":6,"deleted":0}`, where **6 is `Backup/Data`'s file count** and `Backup/Library` held 93. Fixed by reusing `pruneOldBackups` with the same `keepMin` floor, so a quiet month still cannot wipe the only copies. The same PR closed a second gap: the SQLite prune filtered on `f.endsWith('.sqlite')` and therefore abandoned every `-shm`/`-wal` companion, **136 orphans against 26 real backups**. Only 2.1 MB, so the disk was never the harm — the harm is that the directory reported **208 files when 26 were backups**, and that number is precisely what sent me chasing a retention bug that did not exist. I reached two wrong conclusions before finding that `backup.js` had its own prune working correctly all along. **A self-correction worth keeping**: the first patch wrote _two_ mechanisms for one rule — delete a backup's sidecars with it, AND sweep orphans afterwards — and I only noticed because removing the first changed nothing red. The docstring claimed "one rule covers both halves" while the code had two, so the code was collapsed to match the sentence; removing the surviving rule now turns 2 tests red, which the two-mechanism version could not do. **#335 `eb9c8a2` — the retention an admin saves governed nothing.** Settings → Backup persists `retentionDays` and `getStatus()` reports it on the card, but **no prune ever read it**: `getRetentionSettings()` (scheduler prune + prune*library + `import.js`), the `pruneSqliteBackups` call, and `costApi.js`'s save-all snapshot prune all resolved retention from the env var alone, falling back to 30. Five prune operations across three resolution sites, and only the *display* honoured the saved value. On this box persisted and default are **both 30, so the two agreed by coincidence rather than by design** — nothing was ever deleted early, the setting simply had no effect, and it would have surfaced the first time anyone changed it. `getRetentionSettings()` is now the single resolver (persisted → env → 30); `keepMin` has no UI field so it stays env-only. The `costApi` prune was inline in the save-all handler, **untested, and it deletes files**, so it was extracted as `pruneAutoJsonBackups()` to be checkable — `manual*_`snapshots stay exempt because an operator asked for those. **One hunk in that PR is a dedup, not a fix, and says so in the code**: routing`effectiveSettings()`through the shared resolver leaves the suite green when removed, because the local copy it replaced returned the same number in every case; it is kept so one definition of "retention" survives instead of two, and the comment states plainly that deleting the line breaks nothing. **#336`8824a32`— the off-site mirror had never worked, and fixing it leaked a key.** The audit found`~/Library/Logs/ops-offsite-backup.log` reading **`OK: 0, SKIP: 478, ERROR: 0`across 84 days** — the LaunchAgent had fired punctually every four hours since 2026-06-23 and skipped every single time, because its target`/Volumes/OPSBACKUP`was never plugged in. Time Machine reported`No destinations configured`and there were no APFS snapshots, so **every backup sat on`/dev/disk3s1`beside the live`ops.db`**, and the bare-metal-restore runbook's "rsync from `OPS*BACKUP_OFFSITE_DEST`" could not have been executed. Henry chose a LAN destination (`smb://10.102.1.2/Departments$`→`NPI/Henry/ops-control-mirror`) over the drive nobody remembers to connect. **Then I leaked a live key onto that shared drive.** The exclude was written `--exclude '.env'`, which matches only the exact name, so `data/Backup/ops-migration.env`went up carrying all three keys as 64-hex values —`OPS_TOTP_KEY`**identical to the one in use** — and sat on a departmental share for about 13 minutes. Worse, I "confirmed" the exclusions with`rsync --dry-run`**without`-v`**, so rsync printed nothing, I checked an empty list, and I would have reported it clean had I not re-run with output. **The structural half is the part to remember**: the 26 `library*_.tar.gz`files **embed**`Library/Users/users.json`and`sessions.json`, and no filename filter can reach inside an archive — see Lesson 39. Both were removed from the share and the script rewritten to stop trusting its own list: it now sweeps what actually landed (small files for `KEY=<long hex>`, archives for credential filenames), deletes any hit and fails loudly. That sweep earned itself immediately by catching 25 tarballs the exclude list had not accounted for. Two further guards: a **mount sentinel**, because an unmounted SMB share leaves an empty directory behind and `rsync --delete`into that looks like success; and a refusal to run`--delete`unless the destination is the dedicated mirror folder, since a typo would otherwise empty somebody's shared directory. The app side of the same PR stopped`sessions.json`riding along in the tarball at all — live bearer tokens have no restore value, while`users.json` **stays**, because a tarball that cannot restore logins is not a backup — and put off-site health on the Backup card, tracking **`last_ok_at`separately from`last_run_at`**, which is the whole point (Lesson 40). The existing P1-8 test runs **its own copy** of the tar command, so editing `tarLibrary()`without editing the test leaves it green; the new test drives`runBackupCycle`and opens what actually shipped. **Verified on the box, not only in CI**: each PR was merged, built and INSTALLED per Lesson 37 with markers grepped in the installed bundle before and after; the first mirror ran 9m04s for 6 GB (~11 MB/s) and steady-state incrementals take 39s; a real restore drill copied the newest backup off the share and opened it —`integrity_check ok`, 131 quotes / 3058 materials / 15057 ifs_inventory / 54 quote_versions all matching live. **`sqlite3` cannot open a database over SMB** (`unable to open database file`) — not corruption, SQLite simply will not take the file locks on a network share, so any restore must copy to local disk first; this belongs in the runbook. Retention now holds at 25 Library tarballs / 805 MB (from 93 / 2.7 GB), 0 orphan sidecars (from 136), and the mirror carries 4.8 GB / 523 files with **zero** credential files on it by four independent checks. **Operational, not in any PR**: the three files deliberately excluded from the shared mirror (`.env`, `users.json`, `totp_secrets.enc`) were staged to `~/ops-control-secrets/` (`drwx------`, outside both `$SRC`and the repo) with a manifest, a`refresh.command` and a bilingual guide for making an encrypted DMG — Henry types that passphrase, so the encrypted copy is his step, not one this session can take. **Key rotation was considered and declined** after the impact was measured rather than guessed: of five accounts only three (`Administrator`sys,`Hana`and`Jet`admin) would re-enrol, because`OPS_REQUIRE_2FA_ROLES`defaults to`sys,admin`; passwords are argon2id in `users.json`and entirely untouched by`OPS_TOTP_KEY`. The trap is the **order** — rotating the key without quarantining `totp_secrets.enc`first makes`loadTotpSecrets()`return the Sprint-40 fail-closed marker, and the login path's`needsTotp = userHasSecret || secretsUnavailable`then locks out **all five accounts including sys**,`cost`and`viewonly`users who never had 2FA included.`npm run reset-totp` quarantines rather than deletes, which is what keeps that from happening. **Tests**: 34 new across the three PRs (7 retention gaps, 13 resolver + extracted prune, 14 off-site verdict + label + real-tarball); every behaviour test watched failing first, and each fix sabotage-checked so the guard is known to point at the right line. Server 1169 → **1191 pass / 1 chronic skip** (MES-3-FIX-51), client **1959 pass**, ESLint 0 errors, prettier clean, CI 10/10 on all three.
>
> **Sprint S-I18N-WAVES-1-10 — full EN/VI parity across the client, ten waves shipped 2026-09-10 → 2026-09-14 (closing SHA: `5d6dabc` via PR #312).** The 2026-09-10 UI/UX audit left one system half-built: the app shipped a language switch that most screens ignored. Ten waves, one PR per screen family, each with a residual-scan count before and after so "translated" stays a measured claim. **Wave 1 — pricing worksheet** (#292 `62c12e3` header + RFQ Information card + tab strip; #293 `c54fe0b` cost summary strip + worksheet chrome + Complex Calc + 62 `spr.*` sub-product row keys, 3 commits `860cd77`/`aa5e9d0`/`4711827`). Root cause of "one translated tab beside eight English ones": `StandardCalc.jsx:545` had carried `tab.labelKey ? t(tab.labelKey) : tab.label` since Sprint S-PRICING-COMBINED-P1 with a comment promising "all 11 to labelKey-only in a future sprint" — only `combined` ever got a key, and `ComplexCalc` rendered `tab.label` straight at three sites without reading `labelKey` at all. **Wave 2 — tracking screens** (#299 `6b7f9b7`): RFQ Tracker 73 + Sample Tracking 88 + Quote History 27 replacements. The two trackers are near-twins sharing **44 literally identical strings**, so they were translated together into shared `track.*` keys rather than two copies guaranteed to drift; keys split by reach, not by file. **Wave 3 — floating calculators** (#300 `8412838`): Ink Calculator 63 / Print Area 93 / Design Tools + Gallus 124. The grouping is the app's own — `FLOATING_BY_DEFAULT` in `windowLogic.js` — not one invented for the wave. The real finding was not missing translation: all three were **already bilingual and printed both languages at once, to everyone, in every locale**, in three shapes — concatenated literals (`"All · Tất cả"`, `"COLOR CODING / MÃ MÀU Ô"`), twin sibling elements (an English `<div>` beside a `.ink-note-vi`, 7 pairs), and 8 Vietnamese-only strings in Gallus where an English reader saw no English anywhere. **Wave 4 — Material Cost library** (#303 `4d8d0ea`): 191 replacements, 129 keys, **14 components that had no `useI18n` between them**. The obvious assumption — library screens must share vocabulary — **measured false**: 146 distinct strings and only four appear in more than one file (`Add`, `Backup`, `Cancel`, `Delete`, all already generic enough for `common.*`), so the keys are per-file and the work came from volume, not overlap. **Wave 5 — Settings + License Manager** (#304 `87a780c`): 189 replacements, 161 keys, 10 components with no `t`. License Manager was the mirror image of waves 3–4 — it shipped **Vietnamese-only** in #105, so an English reader saw Vietnamese with no English anywhere. Role codes stay and their descriptions move (`SYS — Super Admin` → `SYS — Quản trị tối cao`): SYS/ADMIN/COST/USER/VIEW are what the permission matrix _stores_ and what people say out loud. **Wave 6 — window titles** (#305 `00eceb8`): `tabTitle()` fed the browser tab, the window titlebar and the taskbar from `TAB_TITLES`, a **second map of English strings** beside the sidebar's own `labelKey`s — and the two had drifted (sidebar "Danh sách RFQ" vs titlebar "RFQ List"). It now derives from the sidebar catalog, `TAB_LABEL_KEYS` built from `COST_SECTIONS`, and all 28 `TAB_TITLES` ids already had a `labelKey` so no gap was papered over. **The bug nearly shipped**: translating at store time looks simpler and is wrong — a title is captured when a window _opens_, so switching language would leave every already-open window in the old language, which nobody would call a bug, they would call it "the translation doesn't work". Titles resolve **at render** from `tabId`; `window.title` survives only as the pre-React-mount fallback the window manager needs when hydrating a saved layout before any provider exists. **Wave 7 — Standard Calc Legend** (#306 `973ab62`): the 4,300-line training manual was authored bilingually (`BiRow en= vi=`, `nameVi`, `titleVi`, `bodyVi`) but **rendered both halves stacked** — every heading, note and table cell read twice, and the language switch changed nothing. `Callout`/`Formula`/`BiRow`/`BiHead` now read the active locale from a `LegendI18n` context and render one half via `pickLang`; the shown text gets the primary style (Vietnamese is no longer small grey italics). `CalcLegend.lint.test.js` was watched failing on **81 items** before the fix. **Wave 8 — quote output** (#308 `e5ef416`, replacing #307 which GitHub auto-closed when #306's branch was deleted on merge — a closed PR cannot be reopened or retargeted): Cost Breakdown Std and Cpx are twins of the same layout and both were half translated — the Selling /unit table used `pricing.*` keys while the Target /unit table right beside it and the whole Detailed Breakdown block were hard-coded English; one shared `cb.*` set now backs both so they cannot drift again. **Formal Quotation deliberately chrome-only**: `handlePrint` is `window.print()` and the print stylesheet hides only `.fq-toolbar`, so every label below the toolbar reaches the customer — translating the field labels would hand a Vietnamese quotation to an international customer whenever the operator's UI happened to be in Vietnamese. **Wave 9 — Help tab** (#310 `9a22925`): the same stacked-twin defect one screen over, all 60 entries reading twice. A second bug surfaced on the way — `asText()` returned `v.vi || v.en` **unconditionally**, so formula names and expressions, `meaning`, key-field names, labels and types showed **Vietnamese to English users regardless of locale**. 53 `help.*` chrome keys; entry copy **stays** in `src/help/content.js` because `scripts/help/build-user-guide.mjs` reads the same file for the offline Word export (verified: still builds, 9 sections / 60 entries); search still reads **both** halves so a Vietnamese operator can find an English field name, pinned by a test; 13 twin CSS classes collapsed; `pickLang` promoted from `StandardCalc/legendLang.js` to `utils/pickLang.js` now that it has a second consumer. **Wave 10 — the grids operators use all day** (#312 `5d6dabc`): the Materials · Inks · Processes grids that make up the Materials & Process tab, and the whole Balancing sub-tab, had **no `useI18n` at all** — the densest data-entry surface in the app, entirely English. 126 keys (`cgrid.*` + `bal.*`), residual scan **41 / 34 / 36 / 55 → 0**; the column names the Complex sub-product row already owned (`spr.row`, `spr.usage`, `spr.width`, `spr.speed`, `spr.ref_price`, `inkc.mesh_spec`, …) are **referenced, not re-added**, so the Standard and Complex grids cannot drift apart. **Glossary rule, now written into `costing.js` instead of hiding inside one file's examples**: keep the trade term, translate the ordinary words around it (`Layout Tab` → `Tab Layout`, `Lead time & Notice` → `Lead time & Ghi chú`) — sourced from the 240 bilingual pairs `CalcLegend.jsx` already carried, and confirmed with the NPI manager on 2026-09-10 that MOQ / EAU / Tier / Coverage stay English, alongside QPA, LM, UPH, IFS, DRW, Pitch, Offcut, Slit, Cav., Eff%, Clicks, Setup LM. **Column heads and tooltips get opposite treatment, both written down in the code**: a column head is translated only when Vietnamese has a word of comparable length — Vietnamese is longer than English for most short nouns and a column head sets the column width, so a longer head wraps or pushes the numeric cells off screen, which is worse for the operator than a term they already read all day; every tooltip is translated, because tooltips float and there is no layout to break, and they are the only place these screens explain themselves. **The guard that was not guarding** (found in wave 2): `strings.lint.test.js` enforces en+vi on every key, but it **hard-coded five domain imports** — the new `domains/quality.js` (the SAP-QM analogue `sales.js` had pointed at since v1.3 H2 without the file existing) was never linted at all, so a missing `vi` would have shipped with a green suite. Registration is a side effect of import, so an unimported domain is invisible twice over: **absent at runtime AND unchecked by the guard meant to catch exactly that**. The import list is now read off disk and a new test asserts `main.jsx` imports the same set — verified failing for the right reason by deleting the `quality.js` line and watching it go red, because a guard nobody has seen fail is not yet a guard. **Two follow-ups caught only by grepping the INSTALLED bundle (Lesson 37), never by the suite**: #309 `6377e33` — CalcLayout's MD · TD glossary styled its Vietnamese lines with `.cl-bi-vi`, a class defined **only in `CalcLegend.css`**, a stylesheet CalcLayout never imports; it worked by accident because both files landed in the same bundle, and wave 7 broke it twice over by renaming the class **and** making the Legend a lazy chunk (fix: the glossary owns `.cl-mdtd-vi` in `StandardCalc.css`, the stylesheet its own tab loads, plus a test that CalcLayout borrows no CalcLegend-only class and does not import that stylesheet); #314 `24b3a59` — `SubProductRow.jsx` still had all three add-row buttons hard-coded even though wave 1 had translated the other 73 `t()` calls in that file, fixed by reusing wave 10's shared `cgrid.*.add` keys, which is exactly what shared keys are for. **Tests**: client suite 1797 at plan time → 1874 at wave 10 → **1919/1919 green on `main`**; `strings.lint` 4/4 every wave; ESLint 0 errors throughout (inline-style warnings on the wave-10 files went 90 → 63 as `title="…"` literals became `t()` calls); `vite build` + perf budget green. **Kept English on purpose**: engine field names, DDL keys, machine names, ink and tool types, the Layout-tab field labels (they must match what is on screen), the Formal Quotation document body, and `Installation ID` / `Tier` / `heartbeat` / `mint-license` in License Manager — the words the licence workflow and `mint-license.command` already use, usually open beside the operator reading that table.
>
> **Sprint S-EXPORT-PICKER-POLISH — workbook restructure + material picker + Cost Breakdown search, shipped 2026-09-14 (closing SHA: `78a8c7d` via PR #319).** Nine PRs in one session, every one of them driven by Henry operating the shipped app and reporting what he saw. **Workbook restructure** (#301 `ce554eb`): Henry supplied a target workbook whose `_Audit` sheet is **byte-identical** to a current export's — same `exported_at` to the millisecond, same `payload_sha256` — so it was that export restructured by hand in Excel, not output from another version, which made it a specification rather than a suggestion. Eleven sheets collapse to five visible (`00 Cover`, `01 Layout`, `02 Summarize`, `03 Balancing`, `04 Pricing Snapshot`) plus the two hidden MVP-2 sheets: RFQ MOQ / Materials / Inks / Processes / Pack Ship / Cost Breakdown all become **sections** of one `02 Summarize` sheet, Layout + Balancing + Pricing Snapshot renumber, and `09 Summary` is dropped. The six builders became section builders with the signature `(sheet, startRow, ctx)` returning the next free row — the same tables at an offset, not reimplementations. **#302 `90e0a8c`** then deleted the orphaned `sheets/09-summary.js` and its three now-unused i18n keys, spotted while grepping the **installed** bundle to confirm the drop had actually shipped. Dead code in `sheets/` is worse than dead code elsewhere: that directory reads as a list of what the workbook contains, so a builder sitting in it implies a sheet no export produces, and the next reader either wires it back up or spends time working out why it is not wired. **RFQ required-field gate** (#311 `7bb435c`): a quote could reach Cost Breakdown with an empty USD rate and nothing would fail — it just produced wrong numbers quietly, because `usd_rate = 0` zeroes the Selling and Target VND mirrors, a blank EAU or Product lifetime collapses the `eau × 0.8` tooling cap so tooling amortises over ~1 piece, and `moq = 0` divides every setup cost. The four fields now draw the existing `sc-input-warn` underline, and the RFQ tab refuses to hand over to another sub-tab while any is blank, via a pure `gateSubTabChange(current, next, entryTabId, state)` in `calcValidation.js` that Std calls with `'header'` and Cpx with `'project'` — one rule, neither component re-deriving it. **Scope held deliberately**: the gate governs navigation ONLY; `validateStandard` / `validateComplex` return exactly what they did before (pinned by a test), because making `usd_rate` save-blocking would also have blocked re-saving the 131 existing quotes that predate the rule — a bigger change than was asked for. **Material picker** (#313 `8c1629c` → #315 `f29a0ee` → #316 `e2a35a0`): the right-click picker showed a fixed 5 columns for every library while the NPI library carries 12, so when choosing between two protective films the operator could not see thickness, surface, adhesive, MOQ or lead time — the fields the choice actually turns on — and had to leave the quote, open NPI Materials, look, and come back. Columns are per-library now via `PICKER_COLUMNS`, with a test asserting **every declared column key is actually produced by that library's normalizer**, so a typo fails the build instead of rendering a blank column; the pick-shape `{code, ifs_code, desc, g_price, supplier}` is pinned explicitly rather than trusted to the refactor, because that is what lands in the quote. #315 and #316 are both **my own regressions, caught by Henry's screenshots**. #315: my new `.libp-table-cols th { position: relative }` rule silently un-stuck the header — same cells, later in the file, equal specificity — when sticky is itself a positioning context and the resize grip never needed `relative` at all; plus `nowrap` + `width: max-content` pushed twelve columns off the right edge so no row was ever fully visible, the opposite of the point. #316 is the more instructive one: **the sticky rule was never the problem**. Sticky sticks to the nearest _scrolling_ ancestor, and `.op-modal-body` is `overflow-y: auto` but **not** a flex container, so `flex: 1` on the wrap was inert — the wrap grew to content height, the modal body did the scrolling, and the header went with it. The CSS test was rewritten to pin the **contract** (body flex column + `overflow: hidden`; wrap `overflow: auto` + `min-height: 0`) instead of the declaration, because the declaration was already right while the behaviour was broken. Also landed there: per-column `prio` so optional columns drop out below 900px / 640px measured on the **card** via `ResizeObserver` — the modal is resizable and maximizable, so the viewport is the wrong thing to measure — and a ▦ Columns menu persisted per library, with `visibleColumns()` deciding once so header, body and `<colgroup>` can never disagree. **Export correctness** (#317 `82c1797`): two faults from Henry's exported file. `#NAME?` in Draw Materials because a cell starting with `-` is parsed by Excel as a formula, so `- M-Primary` evaluated as one. Fixed at BOTH ends — the bullet became `•`, which is not a formula character and reads better anyway (removing the cause rather than papering over it), AND `csvEscape` now neutralises any cell still leading with `=`, `+`, `-` or `@`, leading whitespace included since Excel skips whitespace before deciding. That second half closed a live **CSV-injection** vector: a material description typed as `=HYPERLINK(...)` was executable until then. Numbers stay exempt, so `-12.5` is still `-12.5`. The same PR fixed export scope: Summarize flattens a quote into one row per MOQ tier, so ticking a row exported a single tier and "export the RFQ" meant exporting once per MOQ and stitching files. Selecting any tier now exports **every** tier of that quote, in tier order, while rows the current filter hides stay hidden — the export never writes something not on screen. **Cost Breakdown search** (#318 `ae84d26`): typing an RFQ number returned nothing while that RFQ sat in rows 1 and 2 of the unfiltered table. The shared filter reads `r.rfq_number`, but Summarize builds FLAT rows and keys the number **`rfq_no`** (`Summarize.jsx:523`), so `r.rfq_number` was `undefined` on every row and the query could never match — invisible on Quote History and the Approvals inbox because those pass `quoteAccessor`, which does expose `rfq_number`. Diffing every field the filter reads against what the row builder actually sets then turned up the **identical mistake in the date branch** — found by auditing, not by report. **#319 `78a8c7d`**: the RFQ form has both a Direct CU PN and an End CU PN but only End reached the file; `direct_cu_pn` had always been populated by the row builder (`Summarize.jsx:528`) and was simply missing from the column config, so it neither rendered nor exported. Added immediately after Direct Customer so each customer sits beside its own part number, matching the RFQ form's own grouping. The existing 37-key shape guard fired exactly as designed and was bumped to 38 **with the reason recorded** next to the other column-add history rather than silently edited. `vat_loss` and `delivery_term` stay out — widening every export uninvited was not what was asked. **Tests**: client suite 1874 → 1911 across the nine PRs; server 1079/1079 after the workbook restructure; ESLint 0 errors, `vite build` and perf budget green throughout.
>
> **Sprint S-LICENSE-FLEET — License Manager ships, then three faults found in LIVE data, 2026-09-10/11 (SHA: `334cb44` via PR #105).** The long-open v1.6 licence tab — offline-signed, online-distributed — finally landed, closing the "PR #105 license-manager v1.6" WATCH item carried since the D-21 audit. **The merge needed a deliberate conflict resolution, recorded on the PR**: the read-only `window.ops.license.{status,fingerprint}` preload bridge had already been hand-copied onto the single-session branch (commit `64af4af`) so it could ship before go-live, so those two hunks conflict against main and **both halves must be kept** — #105 only adds `applyFromFleet` at those same two points and must not drop what single-session added. Three follow-ups landed the next day, and the thing worth recording is that **every one was found by looking at live data or a screenshot, not by a test**. **#296 `b58528a`** — `m.last_seen?.slice(0, 16).replace('T', ' ')` slices the STORED ISO string, which is UTC, so a heartbeat recorded at 13:19:58 Vietnam time displayed as `2026-09-11 06:19`, seven hours early. `Last seen` is the one column an operator reads to judge whether a machine is still alive, so this is not cosmetic: a healthy machine that looks seven hours stale gets chased, and a genuinely dead one hides inside the same margin. `formatLastSeen()` joined the other pure view helpers in `licenseFleetView.js` so it is unit-tested rather than inlined in JSX. Two choices worth keeping: **`sv-SE` is not a language choice** — it is the locale whose date format is already ISO-8601, so the column stays sortable by eye and keeps the shape of the `Hết hạn` column beside it; and `timeZone` is **injectable** so the assertions do not depend on where CI runs, with one case deliberately in a zone WEST of UTC (`America/Los_Angeles`) where the same instant rolls the **date** back a day — the worse half of this bug class, and the half a Vietnam-only test would never catch. `Hết hạn` was left alone on purpose: that field is a date, not an instant, and the licences carry `T00:00:00Z`, so converting would shift some of them a day for no gain. **#297 `f87a18c`** — four items from reviewing the shipped table against live data. `days_left` used `Math.floor` in both directions, which is the cautious direction while a licence is **valid** ("270d" meaning _at least_ 270 whole days) but **inverts once it has expired**: a row lapsed 85.20 days displayed "expired 86d ago". One rounding rule, two opposite meanings. It now truncates toward zero, with `Math.trunc(-0.4) === -0` normalised rather than leaked into the API response — a test pins that, since `-0 !== 0`. The table also only ever grew: a decommissioned or re-imaged machine stayed forever, and the live table carried a nameless trial row last seen three months earlier, every dead row diluting the one question the table exists to answer. `DELETE /:installation_id` is **sys-only and deliberately NOT `requireOwnMachine`** — this is administration of OTHER machines, the same boundary `/upload` sits behind, and gating it on the caller's own machine would be exactly backwards since you never delete your own row. **#298 `6b8680c`** — found in live data: two sessions for the same user, one from `10.102.3.252` and one from `localhost`, both alive and neither aware of the other. Every web client reported the constant `installation_id: 'web'`, and `findUserSessionConflict` (`authService.js:923`) skips any session whose installation_id equals the caller's — a rule that is correct for re-login and multiple tabs, but with a constant identity **every browser in the company is the same machine**, so the takeover prompt could never fire between two web users and the single-session guarantee simply did not apply on the web surface. `webClientId()` mints a stable per-browser id in localStorage, and the **`web-` prefix is load-bearing**: desktop fingerprints are 64-hex and `licenseFleet`'s `requireOwnMachine` gates on exactly that shape, so a 64-hex web id would let any browser act as a fleet machine and quietly undo #105's own fix. Two tests hold that line, one looping **fifty** minted ids asserting none matches `/^[0-9a-f]{64}$/i`. **Shipped in the same window, independent of the licence work — IFS Full Inventory import** (#284 `d4b4145`, #285 `7f633e2`). #284: operator reported UNIT COST rendering a truncated `Zero Cost Forbid…` across all 9,096 records and PART DESCRIPTION showing `"Finished Goods"` — an accounting classification, not a description. Four investigation steps, **each overturning the one before**, established it was NOT the importer (the same pipeline imported 2,167/2,167 numeric unit prices for `raw_materials`, and no code in the repo knows IFS column names — `raw_json` is stored verbatim against the file's own headers) but the header alias map: run against the real 29-header export, **only 2 of 29 headers matched** (Part No and Site). Same class as Lesson 32 — exact-equality alias matching against real-world export headers. #285: after #284 the screen still looked empty, yet the data was correct — the CSV itself carries **184 junk rows at the end** (positions 8512–8695), each holding only `Inventory Value (USD) = 0`. They imported as records, sorted to the top, and two pages of dashes read as "the import broke" while 8,512 real rows had landed perfectly. The dataset already declared `requiredHeaders: ['Part No']`, but the pipeline only ever checked whether the **column** survived alias mapping (`!(h in mapping)`), never whether a **row** carried a value for it — and a row with no natural key is not a record. `dropKeylessRows()` applies at preview **and** at commit, because `stage` comes from the client and cannot be trusted to have been filtered already. **#294 `ebb5953`** retired the `__probe__` sentinel from four documents that still described it as the live mechanism after S-FIRSTRUN-IPC removed `webRequest.onBeforeRequest` interception from the codebase; the support runbook was the harmful one, telling whoever answers the phone to reason about a CORS block in a probe path that no longer exists. It is also the PR that wrote this file's S-FIRSTRUN-IPC entry. **The superseded S-WIZARD-CORS entry got a pointer, not a rewrite** — editing it to say the right thing would hide that the first fix was wrong, which is the part worth keeping, because the same opaque-origin trap is still available to the next person who reaches for a `data:` URL. Near-miss recorded on that PR: `Sprint S-WIZARD-CORS (PR #133)` appears **twice** in the runbook and a blanket replace would have annotated both — the second sits under `ERR_INVALID_URL`, Bug B of the same sprint, whose boot-time `firstRunCompleted` recovery guard #279 never touched and which is still live at `desktop/main.js:914`.
>
> **Sprint S-UIUX-AUDIT-2026-09-10 — audit of the RUNNING app → five task-by-task plans → all five shipped 2026-09-10/11 (plans committed at SHA: `9a32bdb` via PR #283).** An end-to-end walk of the running app (not the source) surfaced findings across **six subsystems** — window manager, validation layer, CI config, design tokens, i18n, warehouse data. Deliberately split into **five independent plans** under `docs/plans/superpowers/plans/` rather than one combined plan nobody would execute; each stands alone with test-first steps and ships on its own. All five now carry `STATUS: SHIPPED`. **Plan 0 — lint ratchet** (#280 `33c3a40`, #281 `996dcf9`, corrected by #295 `b5d7998`). The Lint job had been red on **every** `main` run since 2026-08-18 — six consecutive — on exactly **one** error: a dead store at `WindowManagerContext.jsx:65` (`let stored = null` that both the `try` and the `catch` overwrite before any read). 413 warnings were under the 435 budget, but `--max-warnings` does not govern errors, so that single error was enough, and every open PR inherited the red including PRs that never touched `client/`. It survived a month because `no-useless-assignment` is control-flow-based and **fires on CI's Node 22 but not on local Node 24**. **Corrected 2026-09-28**: not V8 either — `no-useless-assignment` is in `@eslint/js` 10's recommended set and not 9's, and locally the client config resolves 9.39.4 (S-SAVE-GUARD, CI signal section). #281 then ratcheted the budget 435 → 413, the real CI count, documented as **ratchet-only-down**: a fixed ceiling turns today's debt into a permanent floor, and **303 of those 413** warnings are `no-restricted-syntax` — the unfinished design-token migration — so the 22-warning headroom was not neutral, it was working against the migration. Measured on `client/src`: **965 `style={{`, 768 hardcoded hex, versus 87 `var(--token)`** ≈ 9:1 against the system; `semantic-tokens.css` names its own cause in its header ("additive, not replacement… migrate opportunistically"). i18n stayed healthy because it has a lint test; the tokens rotted because the migration was opt-in. **#295 is the correction worth reading**: the ratchet comment blamed Node 22 vs Node 24, and the _number_ was right (local under-reports by ~60) but the **_cause was wrong, which is worse than no explanation_** because it sends whoever chases it at V8. The real cause is `eslint-plugin-react-hooks` installed **twice** — root `node_modules` at 7.1.1 implements `react-hooks/set-state-in-effect`, `client/node_modules` at 7.0.1 does not; both `package.json` files declare `^7.0.1` and the two lockfiles resolved differently — and the lint job runs `npm ci` at the **root only** (`ci.yml:56`), so CI never sees `client/node_modules`. Proven on one machine, one Node, one commit, two clean worktrees differing only in that link: root-only **413** (CI's exact number for `main`), plus `client/node_modules` **353**. A developer who has run `cd client && npm ci` silently loses every hit from that rule. **Plan 1b — validation on touch** (#282 `ed63dad`). A brand-new Standard Calc record opened showing "⚠ 7 errors + 1 warning · [Header] CCL Part Number is required" before a single keystroke, while the same form measured `requiredMarked: 0`, `fieldsWithAriaInvalid: 0`, `fieldsWithAriaDescribedby: 0` across 25 fields — the app named a required field that **no field on the form was marked as** (no asterisk, no `required`, no `aria-required`), so the user had to reason backwards from the message across 25 inputs, and a screen-reader user heard the field name but never why it was wrong. A bar that is always red is a bar people learn to ignore. `WarningBar` re-ran the validator through `useMemo` on **every** state change including the first, and a new record is empty by definition. The fix needed no rewrite because the architecture was already right — the validator is pure and every warning already has a stable `id` — so a `field` key on header warnings links a warning back to the input that produced it. The plan records the correction **only visual verification caught**: filtering to warnings that carry `field` took a new record from 7 down to **4, not 0**; the unit tests did not catch it because they encoded the same wrong spec. **Plan 2 — data grids open maximized** (#286 `b151eb5` → #289 `5e6f3b0` → #290 `641c98b`). Measured live at 1440×900: six windows open, none auto-closing, each **898px = 62% of viewport**, while the widest table needs **3333px** — the operator saw roughly **27% of the table** while **38% of the screen sat empty** as backdrop behind a floating window, in an app whose main content is a 15-column, 9,096-row grid. No new concept was needed (`MAXIMIZE`, `state: 'max'` and the tab-id `Set` idiom all existed); only the **initial state** of some windows changed, keeping `prevRect` so Restore has somewhere to go. Reading the code turned up a separate bug: the singleton branch of `OPEN` forced `state: 'normal'`, so **re-clicking a sidebar item shrank an already-maximized window**. #289 then fixed the _shape_ of the first fix — #286 used an **allowlist** covering 9 of 28 tabs and the operator immediately reported 13 screens still floating, so the rule was **inverted**: `FLOATING_BY_DEFAULT` names the exceptions and everything else maximizes, with a test pinning that a hypothetical **future** tab id maximizes. That inversion matters more than the patch: an allowlist would have silently handed the same gap to whoever adds the next data screen. #290 removed the last two — Pricing (Std) and (Cpx) had been left floating on my own reasoning that "comparing Standard beside Complex is why MDI exists", which was **speculation, not a requirement**; the operator said the opposite (each is a dense multi-tab form whose right edge a 900px window cuts off, and in practice people work one quote at a time). The 4 surviving exceptions (`ink-calc`, `print-area`, `design-tools`, `messages`) share a reason the two pricing screens never had: they are opened **beside** a quote to feed a number **back into** it, so covering the worksheet defeats the purpose. Final split **24 / 28** maximized. Both tests that used `'standard'` as the example of "an ordinary floating window" were repointed at `ink-calc` **with the reason written down** — not loosened to suit the new behaviour; what they assert (a floating window has `state: 'normal'` and `prevRect: null`) is unchanged. **Plan 3 — Carbon convergence** (#287 `73e4a1f`, docs-only, not one line of JSX restyled). Three design languages coexisted: `DESIGN.md` described an **Apple** system (`#000`/`#f5f5f7`, Apple Blue `#0071e3`, SF Pro, 980px pill buttons), the shipped app is **IBM Carbon** (navy, square corners, IBM Plex Mono, `#0f62fe`), and the token file is a **Tailwind** slate/gray scale — with the top hardcoded hex values mixing both palettes. Documentation that does not match the app is more dangerous than none: a newcomer builds faithfully to the Apple spec and creates a **fourth** visual language. So this wave removed only the sources of drift — the Apple spec was **archived, not deleted** (`docs/architecture/design/ARCHIVED-2026-09-10-apple-design-system.md`, with a banner saying it does not describe the running app; it records a real decision that was weighed and set aside), `DESIGN.md` was rewritten short and Carbon pointing at the three real token files, and exactly **one** token was renamed: `--color-slate-900` held `#0f2341` while real Tailwind slate-900 is `#0f172a`. The **value is correct and deliberate** — it unified `#0e2550`, `#162c52`, `#243352`, `#1a3a5c`, as the file itself records — and only the **name** lied; one wrong entry discredits the whole ramp, because a reasonable reader assumes Tailwind values and picks the wrong colour. Renamed `--color-brand-navy`. **Plan 4 — nav + i18n cleanup** (#288 `4a2ac76`, #291 `d508dfe`; its third part became wave 1 of the translation project — see Sprint S-I18N-WAVES-1-10 above). The sidebar showed **"RFQ Tracking"** under _Quoting & Pricing_ and **"RFQ Tracker"** under _Tracking_, with Home's Quick Action saying "RFQ Tracker" — nothing told an English reader which screen would open. **Vietnamese was already correct** (`Danh sách RFQ` / `Theo dõi RFQ`), so English followed Vietnamese instead of inventing new words: **RFQ List** and **RFQ Progress**, applied to all seven surfaces carrying the old labels (`nav.tab.*`, the `TopBar` breadcrumb, the `CostModule` MDI window title + taskbar, `rfq_tracking.title`, `home.qa.rfq`, `RfqTracking.jsx` `datasetLabel`, `help/content.js`). **Tab ids left alone** — they are serialized into saved window layouts, so renaming an id orphans a user's layout. The PR states plainly that its accompanying test **passes both before and after**: two labels are _confusable_, not _identical_, and no honest automated check defines "confusable" without an arbitrary threshold — the rename is a judgment about wording, not test-driven, written up as such rather than manufacturing a fake red test. #291 then fixed what only looking at the screen could catch: the new login language toggle rendered at the **top-right of the page**, not inside the login card, while every test passed, the build was clean, and #288 had already been declared done. The CSS had been written for `.login-card` — **dead CSS, zero uses in any JSX** — while the real card is `form.cb-card` with `position: static`, so the absolutely-positioned toggle escaped up to the nearest positioned ancestor, `.cb-shell` (`position: fixed`), i.e. the whole page. The plan **did** say to make the card a positioned ancestor if it was not one; it was checked — **on the wrong element**, and the check passed for a class that is never rendered. Fixed with `position: relative` on `.cb-card` plus a comment naming what depends on it, and verified against the real DOM (`cardPosition: relative`, `insideCard: true`, `toggleInsideCardBounds: true`). **Pattern across this sprint worth carrying**: #289, #290 and #291 — and later #309 and #314 in the i18n sprint — were each found by operating the running or installed app, never by the suite. That is exactly what Lesson 37's verify-loop exists for.
>
> **Sprint S-FIRSTRUN-IPC — the CLIENT first-run dialog could never reach the main process, fixed 2026-09-10 (SHA: `d5ed84a` via PR #279).** Henry's CLIENT build answered `✗ Không kết nối được: probe timeout` for every URL, including a SERVER that Safari on the same Mac reached fine. **Root cause**: the dialog is rendered from a `data:text/html` URL, which has an **opaque origin**. Its renderer called `fetch('/__probe__?url=…')` expecting `session.webRequest.onBeforeRequest` to intercept the sentinel — but a **root-relative URL cannot be resolved against a non-hierarchical base**, so WHATWG URL parsing fails, the request is never issued, `onBeforeRequest` never fires and `probeServer()` is never called. The timeout message was the dialog's own 4 s fallback, not a network result. All three buttons (Test / Save / Skip) were dead the same way — S-WIZARD-CORS below had fixed the CORS symptom with a mechanism that cannot work from an opaque origin at all. **Fix**: extract the dialog to `desktop/clientFirstRun.js` and talk over `ipcRenderer.invoke` against three `ipcMain.handle` channels (`ops:firstrun.testServer` / `.save` / `.skip`) — exactly what `setupWizard.js` was already doing correctly. Handlers are removed on `win.on('closed')` so reopening the dialog cannot double-register. **This removes the last `__probe__` / `__firstrun__` sentinel: `webRequest.onBeforeRequest` interception is gone from the codebase.** **Tests**: `desktop/clientFirstRun.test.js` + `desktop/setupWizard.test.js` drive a real `BrowserWindow` in Electron (`test:firstrun` / `test:wizard`) — the first test surface this wrapper has ever had, which is why the dead sentinel survived three months. Also added `markIncomplete()` to `setupWizard.js`, and `clientFirstRun.js` to `desktop/package.json` `build.files` (else it is absent from the packaged app).
>
> **Sprint S-PLANNING-REMOVE — remove the dormant Planning module + Kiosk PWA + Kiosk Admin + Reason Codes shipped 2026-07-22 (SHA: `5e386cc` via PR #245).** CODE-ONLY lean/bundle cleanup — Planning was already gated OFF (`OPS_FEATURE_PLANNING` default off) and unreachable, so this changed no running feature. **DB LEFT INERT**: the Planning tables (`work_order`, `work_order_op`, `wo_code_seq`, `reason_code`, `kiosk_pairing`, `op_status_event`, `idempotency_ledger`) stay in `server/db/schema.sql` untouched — dropping them would migrate the production `ops.db` holding real quotes/materials (unacceptable risk for zero gain). `db/init.js applyAdditiveMigrations` (self-contained `ALTER TABLE` on the inert `work_order_op`) + backup/restore snapshot + live `ops.db` (108 quotes / 2899 materials) all confirmed untouched. **Deleted ~154 files**: `client/src/modules/planning/**`, `domains/planning/**`, `apps/kiosk/**`, `server/routes/planning.js`, `server/services/planningStore.js`, the featureGate harness + tests, the MES seed/migration scripts, and (Henry-confirmed) the `KioskAdmin` + `ReasonCodes` tabs (`reasonCodesApi` too — its backend lived only under `mountPlanning`). **AUDIT-FIRST (Lesson 7) surfaced two couplings the spec's premise missed** — `HomePage.jsx` imported `planningApi` for its WO/order KPI tiles (removed those + the due-soon queue + order-entry quick action, Henry-approved), and `ReasonCodes` was a live Cost tab whose routes were planning-mounted (removed, Henry-approved). Necessary coupling handled: dropped the `OPS_KIOSK_KEY` requirement from `server/index.js` boot + `scripts/preflight-env.js` (else a fresh install fails to boot). **Shared edits** (kept the file, removed refs): App/main/Sidebar/sidebarSections/sectionDefs/strings/api/CostModule/tabPreload/AppConfig (client); `index.js` (mounts + `/api/runtime-config features` now just `alt_materials` + boot check) + `sidebarVisibility.js` server mirror (drift test stays green: client==server ids); package.json (kiosk scripts, `apps/kiosk` in build/test, jest ignores, dead `seed:mes`/`migrate`), `ci.yml` (kiosk step), `desktop/main.js` (`OPS_KIOSK_KEY` gen), `desktop/package.json` (`../domains` extraResources), `eslint.config.js` (kiosk blocks). **Orphans the deletion created** (caught by `deadCode.lint.test.js`): removed `ReasonCodeFormModal`/`ReasonCodeDisableModal` + planning-only `utils/fieldMap.js` + `utils/routingHours.js`(+test); fixed stale `decimalInputBudget` + rbac + http.integration test entries. **Left intentionally** (harmless, no deleted imports): `scripts/import-fallback-xlsx.js` WO-mode (standalone dev script, now permanently gated-off; its test verifies the graceful skip). **Verify**: client 1528/1528, server 1026/1026 (+1 chronic skip), ESLint 0 errors, Vite tree-shook clean (no unresolved), perf-budget green — bundle **shrank ~98 kB (2377→2279) / 12 fewer chunks** (cross-link MES-3-FIX-62). One PR.
>
> **Sprint S-2FA-RESET — SYS-only per-user 2FA reset + Modal focus fix + desktop "Server connecting" screen shipped 2026-07-01/02.** Operators who lose their phone needed a 2FA reset without the all-or-nothing `npm run reset-totp` (nukes everyone). New `POST /api/auth/users/:id/reset-2fa` — `isSys` gate (STRICTER than the legacy admin+ DELETE), step-up password (`checkPassword` on the CALLER, not target), no self-reset, race-safe `updateTotpSecrets` (never raw file ops), revoke target sessions, `TOTP_RESET_BY_SYS` audit; the legacy `DELETE /totp/secret/:username` gained its missing audit row.
